JCS/JPCPHS 2025 Guideline on PH and VTE
I. Introduction
PM?o
This guideline consolidates 3 documents previously issued 
by the Japanese Circulation Society, namely Guidelines for 
the Diagnosis, Treatment, and Prevention of Pulmonary 
Embolism and Deep Vein Thrombosis, Guidelines for the 
Treatment of Pulmonary Hypertension, and Statement 
on the Indications and Practice of Balloon Pulmonary 
Angioplasty for Chronic Thromboembolic Pulmonary 
Hypertension, into a newly revised, integrated volume. 
Although recent advances in the diagnosis and treatment 
of these conditions have been remarkable, they are tightly 
interrelated within the pulmonary circulation. By synthe-
sizing contemporary evidence with the practical insights of 
numerous experts, we aim to provide a comprehensive 
framework for prevention, diagnosis, and therapy across 
the pulmonary vascular field. On that basis, the 3 documents 
have been merged and revised as the 2025 Guidelines for 
Pulmonary Hypertension and Pulmonary Embolism/Deep 
Vein Thrombosis.
The Guidelines for the Diagnosis, Treatment, and 
Prevention of Pulmonary Embolism and Deep Vein 
Thrombosis were first published in 2004 and revised in 
2009 and 2017.
1 The 2017 edition appeared soon after 
direct oral anticoagulants (DOACs) became available, at a 
time when supporting evidence remained limited. However, 
in the intervening years, there have been considerable 
advances in the management of venous thromboembolism 
(VTE) with DOACs, ushering in major changes in practice, 
including outpatient treatment pathways based on DOAC 
monotherapy. In Europe and North America, guideline 
updates have similarly been issued since 2017,
2,3 and 
high
-
quality evidence, including studies originating in 
Japan, has expanded, particularly for unprovoked VTE 
and cancer
-
associated VTE. In addition, given the clinical 
importance of thrombosis in the context of COVID
-
19, 
a full revision was undertaken to incorporate these 
developments.
The Guidelines for the Treatment of Pulmonary 
Hypertension were first published in 1999–2000, underwent 
partial revisions in 2006 and 2012, and were comprehen-
sively revised in 2017.
4 In addition, for balloon pulmonary 
angioplasty (BPA), a therapeutic modality that can be 
regarded as having originated in Japan, a dedicated 
Statement on the Indications and Practice of Balloon 
Pulmonary Angioplasty for Chronic Thromboembolic 
Pulmonary Hypertension was issued in 2014.
5 Since the 
previous revision, the 6th (2018) and 7th (2024) World 
Symposia on Pulmonary Hypertension (WSPH) have been 
convened, and changes to the hemodynamic definition of 
pulmonary hypertension have been proposed.
6 Robust 
evidence has accumulated for pharmacotherapies across 
pulmonary hypertension subgroups, and data, including 
from Japan, have established the role of BPA. Moreover, 
the conceptual approach to treatment stratification has 
evolved from reliance on symptoms captured by the World 
Health Organization (WHO) functional class to pragmatic,

multimodality risk stratification that has become standard 
practice. Because diagnostic and therapeutic paradigms 
have shifted substantially, a comprehensive revision was 
warranted. Notably, among high
-
income countries, this 
document is the first guideline to be issued following the 
proposals of the seventh World Symposium in 2024.
We therefore anticipate that, by incorporating evidence 
generated in Japan, this revision will contribute to advancing 
knowledge internationally in the fields of pulmonary 
hypertension and pulmonary vascular medicine.
2. Top 10 Key Revisions in  
the Present Guidelines
The 10 key revisions in this guideline are listed below.
 1
. 
 One unified, end
-
to
-
end guideline for pulmonary 
circulation
 
   Three legacy documents, namely guidelines for the
 
diagnosis, treatment, and prevention of pulmonary 
embolism and deep vein thrombosis, the pulmonary 
hypertension treatment guideline, and the statement 
on indications and techniques for BPA in chronic 
thromboembolic pulmonary hypertension (CTEPH), 
are fully integrated into a single, coherent 2025 edition 
covering prevention, diagnosis, interventions, and 
longitudinal care across the pulmonary circulation.
 2.
  Adoption of revised definitions and classification of
 
pulmonary hypertension from the WSPH
 
   Proposals from the 6th (2018) and 7th (2024) WSPH,
 
including updated hemodynamic definitions and 
refinements in clinical classification, are taken up and 
contextualized for Japanese practice.
 3.
 Comprehensive remapping of therapeutic algorithms
 
   Across VTE, pulmonary arterial hypertension, and
 
CTEPH, algorithms are rewritten to reflect current 
evidence, risk
-
layered goals (e.g., achieving and 
maintaining a low
-
risk profile in pulmonary arterial 
hypertension), and step
-
up/step
-
across strategies that 
align pharmacotherapy, intervention, and transplant 
referral.
 4
. 
 A new stratification backbone from symptoms to
 
multiparametric risk
 
   The 
pulmonary hypertension treatment architecture 
moves beyond WHO functional class alone to a 
pragmatic, multimodal risk framework using composite 
clinical, biomarker, imaging, exercise, and hemody-
namic signals as treatment targets and decision triggers.
 5.
 BPA elevated and codified with Japan
-
led evidence
 
   Building on a decade of pioneering data from Japan,
 
the role of BPA in CTEPH is expanded and formalized: 
indications, patient selection, procedural conduct, and 
positioning within multimodal therapy are integrated 
into the main treatment algorithms.
 6.
  DOAC
-
centric VTE care with real
-
world outpatient 
pathways
 
   Reflecting rapid evidence accumulation since 2017,
 
the VTE section has been overhauled to bring DOACs 
to the fore and to operationalize safe outpatient 
management models (including “DOAC -only” care) 
where appropriate.
 7
. 
 C
learer management of unprovoked and cancer - 
associated VTE
 
   Recommendations are recast to incorporate the
 
expanded, higher
-
quality evidence base, both from 
Japan and internationally, on recurrence risk and 
anticoagulation intensity and duration in unprovoked 
VTE and malignancy
-
associated VTE.
 8.
 Incorporation of COVID
-
19-related thrombosis
 
   This guideline assimilates data emerging from the
 
pandemic, aligning prevention and treatment of 
COVID
-
19-related thrombosis with contemporary 
VTE practice.
 9
. 
 Broader, practice
-
ready scope: prevention → diagnosis 
→ treatment
 
   The 
guideline deliberately links prevention, early 
detection, and definitive therapy for conditions across 
the pulmonary vasculature, addressing their “tight 
coupling” in daily practice.
10.
 
 First post
-
WSPH
-
7 national guideline, highlighting 
Japanese contributions
 
   As the first comprehensive guideline released after the
 
7th WSPH (2024), this revision of the guidelines 
systematizes data originating from Japan, especially 
with regard to BPA, and is positioned to contribute to 
international standard
-
setting in pulmonary vascular 
medicine.

and Level of Evidence

evidence are categorized as detailed in Tables

II. Definition, Diagnosis, and Testing of Pulmonary Hypertension
PM?c
 ▋ 1.1 Definition of Pulmonary Hypertension
Pulmonary hypertension (PH) is defined as a mean 
pulmonary arterial pressure (mPAP) >20
 mmHg measured
 
in the supine position at rest using right heart catheterization 
(RHC). Importantly, this change in the definition from the 
previous guidelines does not imply a recommendation for 
therapeutic intervention in patients previously classified as 
borderline PH with an mPAP of 21–24
 mmHg. Further
 
evidence is necessary to justify treatment in such patients 
(Recommendation Table
 
1).
To distinguish precapillary PH caused by pulmonary 
vascular disease from elevated mPAP due to left heart 
disease, increased cardiac output (CO; equivalent to 
pulmonary blood flow), or increased intrathoracic pressure, 
it is necessary to include pulmonary vascular resistance 
(PVR) and pulmonary arterial wedge pressure (PAWP) in 
the definition. The 2022 European Society of Cardiology 
(ESC)/European Respiratory Society (ERS) guidelines 
define precapillary PH as PVR >2 Wood units and PAWP 
≤15
 
mmHg.7 The present guideline adopts the same 
thresholds. However, for PAWP, values higher than the 
empirically determined upper limit of 12
 mmHg are
 
commonly used in practice. Therefore, when evaluating 
patients with PAWP between 12 and 15
 mmHg, clinicians
 
should carefully consider risk factors and echocardiographic 
findings of left-sided heart failure. Hemodynamically, 
post-capillary PH is defined as mPAP >20
 mmHg and 
PAWP >15
 mmHg. In such cases, patients with PVR 
≤2 
Wood units are categorized as having isolated post-capillary 
PH, whereas those with PVR >2 Wood units are classified 
as having combined post- and precapillary PH.
 ▋ 1.2 Exercise PH
In healthy subjects, during exercise, mPAP does not exceed 
30
 mmHg when CO is 10
 L/min, or the mPAP/CO slope
 
remains ≤3
 
mmHg/L/min.8 Therefore, exercise PH is defined 
as an mPAP/CO slope >3
 mmHg/L/min during exercise.9 
To assess the proportional increase in mPAP relative to 
CO, one may plot CO on the x -axis and mPAP on the 
y-axis and calculate the slope. Alternatively, the change in 
mPAP from rest to exercise divided by the change in CO 
may be used as a simplified index.
2. Clinical Classification
The purpose of the clinical classification of PH is to 
categorize the clinical conditions associated with PH based 
on similar pathophysiological mechanisms, clinical and 
hemodynamic characteristics, and treatment strategies. This 
guideline adopts the classification proposed at the 7th World 
Symposium on Pulmonary Hypertension (WSPH-7).
10 
Major changes in this revision include:
• 
 the
  classification
  of
  long-term
  responders
  to
  calcium
 
channel blockers in idiopathic pulmonary arterial hyper
-
tension (PAH)11,12
•   the  categorization  of  PAH  with  features  of  pulmonary 
veno-occlusive disease (PVOD) or pulmonary capillary 
hemangiomatosis, and persistent pulmonary hypertension 
of the newborn as subtypes within Group 1 rather than 
separate subgroups
• 
 the
 subclassification
 of
 some
 Group
 2
 and
 3
 PH
 subtypes 
by specific disease names.12
3. Diagnostic Algorithm  
(Recommendation Table
 
2)
The differential diagnosis of patients presenting with 
unexplained exertional dyspnea or suspected PH is broad. 
In some cases, rapid clinical judgment is required, and a 
practical approach based on algorithms is essential. The 
diagnostic (Figure
 
PH????Ge
 
2) 
algorithms aim to facilitate smooth cooperation among 
primary care physicians, regional core hospitals, and PH 
centers capable of specialized diagnosis and treatment, 
enabling comprehensive evaluation at PH centers.
 ▋ 3.1 Primary Care Physicians
Because PH patients typically present with non-specific 
symptoms, it is expected they will first consult primary care 
physicians. Initial evaluations should include medical and 
family history, physical examination (e.g., blood pressure, 
heart rate), chest X-ray, electrocardiogram, blood tests 
such as B-type natriuretic peptide/N-terminal pro B-type 
natriuretic peptide, and oxygen saturation. If abnormalities 
are detected, referral to a regional core hospital should be 
considered.
 ▋ 3.2 Regional Core Hospitals
For patients presenting with suspected PH, non-invasive 
tests are prioritized. Pulmonary diseases are evaluated 
using pulmonary function tests, arterial blood gas analysis, 
and chest computed tomography (CT) if needed. Cardiac 
assessments include echocardiography and cardiopul-
monary exercise testing. Echocardiography plays a central 
role in the diagnostic algorithm and is useful regardless of 
the underlying cause of PH. It is also critical in differentiating 
other cardiac diseases. Based on initial findings, further 
evaluation or referral depends on the likelihood of PH or 
the identification of alternative causes.

 ▋ 3.3 Referral to PH Centers
If PH is moderately to highly suspected, further evaluation 
should be conducted at a specialized PH center. The goal 
is to confirm the differential diagnosis and identify the 
specific cause based on the current clinical classification. 
Urgent referral is especially important when clinical signs 
suggest PAH or chronic thromboembolic pulmonary 
hypertension (CTEPH), because timely intervention can 
significantly impact prognosis. Critical signs include rapidly 
progressive symptoms, World Health Organization (WHO) 
Class III or IV, right heart failure, syncope, intolerance to 
arrhythmia, and hemodynamic instability (e.g., hypotension, 
tachycardia, low CO). A ventilation–perfusion (V/Q) scan 
is the important initial diagnostic test for CTEPH, given 
its high sensitivity. If CTEPH is suspected, pulmonary

4. Diagnostic Tests
This section focuses on diagnostic tests that are relevant to 
the confirmation and classification of PH. Rather than 
providing an exhaustive list of all possible evaluations, key 
investigations are highlighted that play a central role in 
establishing the diagnosis, assessing severity, and guiding 
subsequent management.
 ▋ 4.1 Echocardiography (Recommendation Table  3)
Echocardiography serves as a gatekeeper in PH diagnosis 
because of its ability to estimate pulmonary artery pressure. 
It is recommended for patients suspected of having PH or 
those with comorbidities potentially associated with PH.
13
Because there are multiple underlying mechanisms and 
presentations of PH, various echocardiographic parameters 
are used to assess the likelihood of PH. In addition to 
estimating systolic pulmonary artery pressure, other echo-
cardiographic signs indicating PH, such as those pertaining 
to the ventricles, pulmonary artery, inferior vena cava 
(IVC), and right atrium, are evaluated. Due to potential 
measurement errors in estimated right atrial pressure, peak 
tricuspid regurgitation velocity (TRV) alone is recommended 
as a key variable to estimate the probability of PH. TRV 
can be underestimated in cases of severe tricuspid regurgi-
tation or when the Doppler beam is not aligned with the 
regurgitant jet, and overestimated in high-output conditions 
such as liver disease or sickle cell disease. Furthermore, 
TRV may be inaccurate due to closure artifacts or misiden-
tification of the peak.
13 Therefore, TRV alone cannot rule 
out PH.14
After assessing TRV, additional indicators involving the 
ventricles, pulmonary artery, IVC, and right atrium 
(Table
 
3) should be considered to determine the echocar-
diographic probability of PH as low, intermediate, or high.7 
This classification aids clinicians in deciding the need for 
further detailed examinations, including RHC (Figure
 
3).7 
Although the threshold mPAP has been lowered in the 
definition of PH, there are currently no data supporting a 
corresponding lowering of the TRV threshold.
15,16
For PH patients with concomitant cardiac disease, 
echocardiography is also useful in diagnosing the underlying 
cardiac condition. Shunt-type congenital heart disease must 
not be overlooked. In PH due to left heart failure, especially 
heart failure with preserved ejection fraction (HFpEF), 
assessments of left ventricular diastolic function (e.g., left 
atrial size, left ventricular hypertrophy, Doppler indices)

For the diagnosis of PH, ≥2 findings from categories A–C are required. When assessing PH risk, it is essential to evaluate the 
presence of risk 
factors for left heart disease and CTEPH. Right heart catheterization should be considered depending on symptoms and clinical context.

and valvular disease are important. Other indices of right 
ventricular function, including strain values, are useful 
prognostic markers in PH and may serve as supplementary 
indicators for disease monitoring during medical therapy.
17,18
 ▋ 4.2 Pulmonary Function Testing
Pulmonary function testing is valuable in patients with PH 
primarily for identifying or excluding underlying pulmonary 
diseases. It is essential in differentiating Group 3 PH, such 
as PH due to chronic obstructive pulmonary disease or 
restrictive ventilatory disorders seen in pulmonary fibrosis 
and interstitial lung disease.
In idiopathic or heritable PAH (Group 1), spirometry 
(e.g., percentage vital capacity [%VC] and percentage 
forced expiratory volume in 1 second) is often within the 
normal range. However, many diseases that cause PH are 
associated with reduced pulmonary capillary blood volume, 
resulting in a reduction in the diffusing capacity of the 
lungs for carbon monoxide (DLCO), measured by precision 
pulmonary function equipment.
19 In contrast, in left-to-right 
shunt disorders, DLCO may be elevated, reflecting increased 
pulmonary blood flow. In combined pulmonary fibrosis 
and emphysema, typically considered a “syndrome” rather 
than a distinct disease entity, PH is frequently present. 
Although spirometry results may be normal or only mildly 
reduced, DLCO is markedly decreased.
20 In PH associated 
with systemic sclerosis, a disproportionate reduction in 
%DLCO compared to %VC (i.e., %VC
 /
 %DLCO 
≥1.4–2.0) 
suggests the presence of PH.21
 ▋ 4.3 Ventilation–Perfusion Scintigraphy
A lung perfusion scan is useful in detecting perfusion 
defects and identifying their locations. However, perfusion 
defects can also occur in areas affected by parenchymal lung 
diseases such as atelectasis, emphysema, and pneumonia. 
Therefore, it is important to assess the presence of lung 
parenchymal abnormalities using ventilation scans, chest 
X-ray, or CT in conjunction.
In diseases causing narrowing or obstruction of the 
pulmonary vessels, such as CTEPH or vasculitis, perfusion-
only defects often appear as wedge-shaped areas on 
perfusion scintigraphy. If both ventilation and perfusion 
scans are normal, CTEPH can be effectively ruled out. The 
reported sensitivity and specificity of perfusion scans are 
90–100% and 94–100%, respectively, making them superior 
to contrast-enhanced chest CT in this regard.
22,23 In PAH, 
peripheral patchy perfusion defects or a mottled perfusion 
pattern may be seen, and similar heterogeneous perfusion 
findings can occur in PVOD/pulmonary capillary heman-
giomatosis.
24
 ▋ 4.4 Cardiac Catheterization and Provocation Testing
  ▋ 4.4.1  Right Heart Catheterization ( Recommendation 
Table
 
4)
RHC is needed for the diagnosis, classification, and 
assessment of severity and treatment response of PH. It 
also plays a role in assessing the eligibility of patients for 
heart or lung transplantation and in diagnosing congenital 
heart defects. Because RHC is an invasive hemodynamic 
test, it should be performed after appropriate non-invasive 
evaluation.
The incidence of serious adverse events reported by PH 
centers is 1.1%, with a procedure-related mortality rate of 
0.055%.

month), mechanical right-sided valves, and acute infec-
tions. Pulmonary artery perforation is the most severe 
complication.

>75% and suspected left-to-right shunt, stepwise oxygen 
saturation measurements should be obtained from the 
superior vena cava, IVC, right atrium, right ventricle, and 
pulmonary artery. CO should be measured using either the 
direct Fick method or thermodilution (averaged over at 
least 3 measurements). However, thermodilution may be 
inaccurate in low-output states or severe tricuspid regurgi-
tation.
26,27 The indirect Fick method is considered less 
reliable. In congenital shunt disease, thermodilution-derived 
CO may be inaccurate, and the Fick method is preferred.
28 
Note that CO and pulmonary blood flow are usually 
equivalent, but differ in shunt disease.
All pressure measurements, including PAWP, should be 
performed at end-expiration without breath-holding. In 
patients with significant respiratory swings (e.g., chronic 
obstructive pulmonary disease, obesity, during exercise), 
the average of 3–4 respiratory cycles should be used. If 
reliable PAWP cannot be obtained, consider measuring 
left ventricular end-diastolic pressure via left heart 
catheterization.
  ▋ 4.4.2  Vasoreactivity Testing ( Recommendation 
Table
 
4)
Vasoreactivity testing identifies patients with idiopathic, 
heritable, or drug/toxin-associated PAH who exhibit 
marked hemodynamic improvement with high-dose calcium 
channel blockers.
29,30 In other PAH forms or PH Groups 
2–5, positive responses do not correlate with favorable 
long-term outcomes and are not recommended due to low 
positive rates.
31
Inhaled nitric oxide (10–20 ppm for 5  min)29 or iloprost 
(5–20
 μg for 15
 min)32,33 are recommended agents. Although 
intravenous epoprostenol is also supported by evidence, its 
use is more time-consuming.
29
A positive response (Sitbon criteria) is defined as a ≥10-
mmHg drop in mPAP to ≤ 40
 mmHg with unchanged or
 
increased CO. 33 Although the positive response rate to 
vasoreactivity testing is <10%, those patients who meet the 
criteria may benefit from long-term monotherapy with 
calcium channel blockers and have a favorable prognosis. 
Vasoreactivity testing should be performed at the time of 
initial RHC.
  ▋ 4.4.3 Exercise Testing
Exercise RHC is the gold standard for evaluating exercise
 
PH.8 Exercise testing helps detect early pulmonary vascular 
or left heart dysfunction in patients with normal resting 
hemodynamics who present with dyspnea, genetic predis-

position to PAH, connective tissue diseases, or suspected 
CTEPH.34–38
Exercise testing is typically performed in the supine 
position using a bicycle ergometer, often alongside expira-
tory gas analysis for Fick-derived CO. Load is increased 
via a ramp or stepwise protocol until symptom-limited. At 
each level, measure systemic blood pressure, heart rate, 
pulmonary arterial pressure, PAWP, SaO
2, SvO2, and right 
atrial pressure.
As noted above, the mPAP/CO slope can be calculated 
by plotting CO (x -axis) against mPAP (y -axis). A slope 
>3
 mmHg/L/min indicates exercise PH.
Measuring PAWP during exercise is challenging due to
 
respiratory variation. For accurate PAWP measurement 
and to avoid misclassification, select pressure values near 
end-diastole rather than mean pressure values over the 
cardiac cycle, particularly in patients with large V waves or 
mitral valve disease.
39
  ▋ 4.4.4 Fluid Challenge Test
Compared 
to exercise testing, fluid challenge with normal 
saline avoids intrathoracic pressure changes and does not 
require special equipment, making it a simple and practical 
test. In patients with a PAWP of 13–15
 mmHg, fluid
 
challenge or exercise testing is recommended to avoid 
misclassification.
For fluid challenge, administer 500
 mL (7–10
 mL/kg) of
 
saline over 5–10
 min. A post-challenge PAWP 
≥18
 
mmHg 
is considered positive. 40 Most studies have focused on 
identifying latent HFpEF rather than precapillary PH. 
Passive leg raise or leg positive pressure during RHC can 
also help detect latent HFpEF by increasing preload.
41
 ▋ 4.5 Genetic Testing (Recommendation Table  5)
Pathogenic variants associated with PAH have been 
identified in idiopathic PAH, heritable PAH, drug/toxin-
induced PAH, and PVOD. Affected genes include BMPR2 
and ACVRL1,
42 which are involved in pathways regulating 
pulmonary vascular cell proliferation.
Although the identification of genetic variants does not 
change the therapeutic approach, panel testing can aid 
diagnosis and detect related conditions. If a patient has a 
disease potentially associated with genetic mutations, they 
should be informed about the possibility of a genetic 
contribution. Identifying a pathogenic variant also allows 
for familial risk assessment and early detection.
37,42
Twelve genes are internationally recognized as highly 
relevant to PAH, including BMPR2, ACVRL1, ATP13A3, 
CAV1, EIF2AK4, ENG, GDF2, KCNK3, KDR, SMAD9, 
SOX17, and TBX4.43 In Japan, panel testing is available as 
an out-of-pocket service. 44 ACVRL1 and ENG are also 
causative genes of hereditary hemorrhagic telangiectasia, 
and require evaluation for associated complications. 
Notably, RNF213 variants (e.g., p.Arg4810Lys), related to 
moyamoya disease and pulmonary artery stenosis, have also 
been reported in patients with idiopathic PAH in Japan.
45
5. Risk Stratification for PAH
This guideline proposes a new risk stratification model for 
PAH based on the ESC guidelines used at diagnosis and 
follow-up and the French registry score
46 validated in Japan. 
An mPAP cutoff of 40
 mmHg is included. Although a 4-tier
 
model (splitting intermediate risk into low-intermediate 
and high-intermediate risk) has been proposed in other 
guidelines, Japanese data show no clear prognostic differ-
ence between the high-intermediate and high-risk groups. 
Similarly, the ESC guideline and WSPH-7 do not offer 
distinct therapeutic strategies for these categories.
47 Because 
both groups share the same level of risk and are managed 
similarly, these guidelines adopt a 3-tier classification in 
Japan (Table
 
5).

III. Group 1 Pulmonary Arterial Hypertension
This chapter focuses on therapeutic approaches and 
management strategies of pulmonary arterial hypertension 
(PAH), without addressing general epidemiology or 
etiological aspects.
PM?h??g?o`g
 ▋ 1.1 Treatment
  ▋ 1.1.1 Supportive Therapy
a.
 Anticoagulant Therapy
PAH is known to predispose to microthrombosis due to a
 
hypercoagulable state.48 However, the efficacy and safety 
of warfarin remain unestablished, and routine use is not 
encouraged.
b.
 
Diuretics
Loop diuretics and aldosterone antagonists are administered 
to manage right ventricular preload. For patients on high 
doses of loop diuretics, tolvaptan has been reported to aid 
in dose reduction.
49 Attention is required in patients with 
low cardiac output to avoid low-output syndrome.
c.
 Long-Term Oxygen Therapy
Oxygen therapy exerts acute effects in lowering pulmonary
 
artery pressure and pulmonary vascular resistance (PVR) 
in PAH. Typically, oxygen is administered to maintain PaO2 
levels above 60
 mmHg.
d.
 Iron Deficiency
Iron deficiency is common in idiopathic and heritable PAH
 
(I/HPAH) and is correlated with worsened symptoms 
and higher mortality risk, regardless of the presence of 
anemia.
50–52 Although the benefit of iron supplementation 
in non-anemic patients is uncertain, 53 it may improve 
exercise tolerance and reduce PAH exacerbation-related 
hospitalizations. At a minimum, iron supplementation is 
recommended in patients with anemia.
54,55 Because hepcidin 
levels are elevated in PAH, 56 impairing gastrointestinal 
iron absorption, intravenous iron should also be considered 
in addition to oral iron.
e.
 
Arrhythmias
Supraventricular tachycardias, particularly atrial fibrillation 
and atrial flutter, are frequently observed in PAH, with one 
study reporting a prevalence of 25%.
57 Supraventricular 
tachycardias are particularly common in patients with 
congenital heart disease and are associated with elevated 
right atrial pressure, pulmonary arterial wedge pressure, and 
N-terminal pro B-type natriuretic peptide (NT-proBNP),

suggesting a link with right heart failure. 58 Although 
evidence remains limited, catheter ablation has been 
reported to reduce antiarrhythmic use, B-type natriuretic 
peptide (BNP) levels, and hospitalizations.
59
  ▋ 1.1.2 Targeted Therapy
Rapid reduction of PAP and PVR is essential, because this
 
is expected to suppress pulmonary vascular remodeling 
and reduce right ventricular afterload, ultimately improving 
survival.
60 If the acute vasoreactivity test is positive, high-
dose calcium channel blockers are likely to yield significant 
hemodynamic improvement and should be the first-line 
therapy.
If the vasoreactivity test is negative, pulmonary vasodi-
lators are used. These agents target 3 key pathways: the 
endothelin pathway, the nitric oxide pathway, and the 
prostacyclin pathway. Combination therapy aiming at 
hemodynamic remission is recommended. Table
 
6 presents 
the standard usage of pulmonary vasodilators in Japan.
a.
 Oral Combination Therapy
Initial 
combination therapy with ambrisentan 10
 mg 
and 
tadalafil 40
 mg (AMBITION trial61) or macitentan 10 mg  
and tadalafil 40  m
g (ADUE trial 62) has been shown to 
significantly improve outcomes over monotherapy and is 
recommended for initial treatment in non-high-risk PAH.
b.
 Triple Combination Therapy
For 
high-risk patients or those with an insufficient response 
to initial oral therapy, triple therapy including a prostacyclin 
pathway agent is advised. Adding selexipag (0.4–3.2
 mg) to
 
oral dual therapy has shown superiority over dual therapy 
alone.
63 In Japan, available parental prostacyclins include 
intravenous epoprostenol and inhaled, subcutaneous, and 
intravenous treprostinil. In the era of limited options, ultra 
high doses of epoprostenol up to 200
 ng/kg/min were used.
 
However, in Europe, initial triple therapy using around 
20 ng/kg/min has been shown to elicit marked hemodynamic  
improvements and have a favorable effect on prognosis.64,65
If parental prostacyclin therapy fails to achieve sufficient 
improvement, listing for lung transplantation should be 
considered. Treprostinil is less potent than epoprostenol 
and generally requires 2–3 times the dose to achieve similar 
effects.
66 Some reports suggest that switching from epopro-
stenol to treprostinil may maintain efficacy while reducing 
side effects.
67 Subcutaneous treprostinil has demonstrated 
comparable long-term outcomes to epoprostenol,68 although 
injection site reactions and pain can limit its use.
c.
 Activin Signaling Inhibitors
So
tatercept, an activin signaling inhibitor, has shown 
promise in suppressing pulmonary vascular remodeling. 
A phase III international trial demonstrated significant 
improvement in the 6-minute walk distance,
69 suggesting 
sotatercept may be an effective add-on therapy for patients 
not responding adequately to initial combination therapy.
2. Systemic Sclerosis-Associated PAH  
(Recommendation Table
 
6)
This guideline distinguishes between PAH associated with 
systemic sclerosis (SSc-PAH) and PAH associated with 
other connective tissue diseases (CTDs; non-SSc CTD-
PAH), and describes their respective characteristics. In 
Western countries, SSc-PAH accounts for over 60% of 
cases of CTD-PAH,
70,71 whereas in East Asia, including 
Japan, non-SSc CTD-PAH is relatively more prevalent.72,73 
This revision of the guideline reflects the observation that 
immunosuppressive therapy, in addition to vasodilators, 
may be effective in non-SSc CTD-PAH.
Recommendation Table 6. Recommendations and Levels of Evidence for the Diagnosis and Treatment of 
SSc-PAH
COR LOE
It is recommended to annually assess the risk of PAH in patients with SSc I B
 It is recommended to use the DETECT algorithm to screen for asymptomatic PAH in adult SSc 
patients with disease duration >3 years, FVC ≥40%, and %DLCO <60% I B
 It is recommended to perform RHC to exclude PAH in patients with SSc who have unexplained 
dyspnea despite non-invasive testing I C

or pulmonary function tests with BNP or NT-proBNP measurement based on dyspnea evaluation IIa B

 ▋ 2.1 Diagnosis and Screening
Annual screening is recommended in all patients with 
systemic sclerosis (SSc), regardless of symptoms. Active 
screening has been shown to increase the proportion of 
patients diagnosed in World Health Organization (WHO) 
Class I/II and improve survival.
74,75 Screening accuracy 
improves when transthoracic echocardiography is combined 
with pulmonary function tests and biomarkers (BNP/NT-
proBNP), helping avoid unnecessary right heart catheter-
ization (RHC) and detect early PAH (mean pulmonary 
arterial pressure [mPAP] 21–24
 mmHg).76–78
The DETECT algorithm targets asymptomatic SSc 
patients with disease duration >3 years, forced vital capacity 
≥40%, and diffusing capacity of the lungs for carbon 
monoxide (DLCO) <60%, and offers higher sensitivity 
than transthoracic echocardiography alone.
76 Additional 
tools to support RHC indication include exercise echocar-
diography, cardiopulmonary exercise testing, and cardiac 
magnetic resonance imaging.
79–83 However, it should be 
noted that most evidence for these methods was gathered 
???o`o?≥QT
 
mmHg definition.
 ▋ 2.2 Treatment
Treatment of SSc-PAH follows the principles for I/HPAH. 
Immunosuppressive therapy, often considered in non-SSc 
CTD-PAH, is generally ineffective in SSc-PAH and is not 
recommended.
84 In patients with concomitant left heart 
disease or interstitial lung disease, consider initiating 
monotherapy with either a phosphodiesterase 5 (PDE5) 
inhibitor or endothelin receptor antagonist, following the 
approach for PAH with cardiopulmonary comorbidities.
If pulmonary veno-occlusive disease (PVOD)/pulmonary 
capillary hemangiomatosis (PCH) is suspected based on 
%DLCO <50%, hypoxemia, or high-resolution computed 
tomography (CT) findings (interlobular septal thickening, 
centrilobular ground-glass opacities, mediastinal lymph-
adenopathy), pulmonary vasodilator therapy should be

DPAH, and SSc-PAH

 It is recommended to administer high-dose calcium channel blockers in patients with a positive

 It should be considered to reassess hemodynamics 3–4 months after initiating high-dose calcium 
channel blocker therapy in patients with a positive acute vasoreactivity test, and to consider 
further therapeutic interventions if improvement is insufficient
IIa B
 It is recommended to perform multimodality assessment, including hemodynamic evaluation, and 
risk stratification based on long-term prognostic indicators at the time of diagnosis of IPAH, 
HPAH, DPAH or SSc-PAH
I B

HPAH, DPAH or SSc-PAH

 It is recommended to initiate triple combination therapy consisting of parenteral prostacyclin 
analogs (epoprostenol or treprostinil), an ERA, and a PDE5 inhibitor in high-risk patients at the

interventions in patients with IPAH, HPAH, DPAH or SSc-PAH who are assessed as intermediate- 
or high-risk during follow-up

have similarly focused on typical PAH with pulmonary 
vascular involvement.
86 Therefore, the treatment algorithm 
based on risk stratification is designed specifically for these 
patient groups, as shown in Figure
 
4.47 For risk assessment,

Primary risk stratification follows the Japanese Circulation 
Society (JCS) risk stratification tool (Table
 
5), but alternative 
tools, most notably those in the 2022 European Society of 
Cardiology (ESC)/European Respiratory Society (ERS)

Prior to applying the algorithm, it is essential to evaluate

10 Because some

 ▋ 3.2  Treatment of PAH With Cardiopulmonary 
Comorbidities (Recommendation Table
 
8)
The number of PAH patients with comorbid conditions is 
increasing in clinical practice. In the GRIPHON trial,
87 
51% of all patients had at least one cardiovascular 
comorbidity; in the AMBITION trial,
88 40% of patients 
had hypertension, 10% had diabetes, and 4% had coronary 
artery disease. Although pulmonary vasodilators are 
effective in these patients, the benefits of early combination 
therapy, which are seen in typical PAH, may not be as 
significant. In addition, the incidence of adverse events 
during treatment is higher. In patients with lung diseases, 
adverse events such as hypoxemia are more frequent, 
requiring more frequent follow-up.
Conversely, the GRIPHON and AMBITION trials 
showed that the reduction in the risk of clinical worsening 
among patients with comorbidities was similar to that seen 
in the overall study populations. Moreover, patients with

appropriate treatment intensification remains important.
Based on the above, initial treatment in PAH patients 
with comorbidities should start with monotherapy. Careful 
monitoring of treatment tolerability and response is 
essential, and combination therapy should be considered if 
monotherapy is insufficient. However, during the course of 
treatment, it may become evident that PH is driven 
predominantly by mechanisms other than Group 1 PAH. 
In such cases, it is appropriate to taper or discontinue 
PAH-specific treatments and instead focus on treating the 
underlying disease, such as left heart disease.
90 Treatment 
of the coexisting lung or heart disease should also be 
provided in parallel (Figure
 
5).
 ▋ 3.3 Initial Treatment Algorithm
Initial treatment should be based on risk stratification. In 
Japan, triple combination therapy is actively used in severe 
cases, with favorable outcomes.
91 Moreover, data from 
international registries suggest that triple therapy, including 
parental prostacyclin analogs,
65 is effective for high-risk 
patients and should be promptly initiated after diagnosis.
For patients not classified as high risk, initial combination 
therapy with an endothelin receptor agonist and a PDE5 
inhibitor is recommended. Although both drugs may be 
started on the same day, sequential initiation over 1–2 
weeks is often preferred to ensure tolerability. Tadalafil 
combined with macitentan (ADUE trial
62) or ambrisentan 
(AMBITION trial) are both supported by evidence and 
recommended. Sildenafil is typically administered at 20
 mg
 
orally 3 times daily; 61 although doses up to 80  mg have

When using parental prostacyclin analogs, it is common

is summarized in Table
 
6.
 ▋ 3.4 Follow-up Interval and Treatment Intensification
After initiating therapy, hemodynamic reassessment is 
generally performed after 3–4 months to evaluate treatment 
response. If treatment is escalated thereafter, follow-up 
should continue every 3–6 months. Once the patient has 
reached and maintained a low-risk and stable status, 
follow-up may include non-invasive risk assessments every 
6–12 months, along with annual hemodynamic evaluations, 
although catheterization intervals may be extended in 
cases of excellent hemodynamic stability.
47
Risk assessment should be repeated during follow-up. In 
principle, treatment intensification is not required for low-
risk patients. However, meta-analyses of the AMBITION, 
GRIPHON, and SERAPHIN trials show that over 20% 
of patients categorized as low-risk experience clinical 
worsening within 3 years.
95 Thus, even for low-risk patients, 
individualized treatment escalation cannot be ruled out.
In Japan, treatment goals include achieving mPAP 
≤40
 mmHg or pulmonary vascular resistance (PVR) <5
 
Wood units (based on the REVEAL registry96), because these 
thresholds are associated with improved right ventricular 
function and long-term outcomes.
97–99 Consequently, treat-
ment intensification aiming for these goals may be widely 
accepted in the future.
For intermediate-risk patients already on dual oral therapy, 
additional treatment with oral or inhaled prostacyclin 
analogs (e.g., selexipag, treprostinil) may be considered. In 
the GRIPHON study, most patients received background 
oral therapy, establishing selexipag as a well-supported 
third-line agent.
100 Transitioning from a PDE5 inhibitor to 
riociguat is also possible based on an open-label randomized 
controlled trial, although that study was unblinded and 
used unvalidated endpoints, and thus carries a lower 
recommendation grade.
101 For patients already receiving 
parental prostacyclin therapy, dose escalation may be 
warranted; however, optimal target dose data are limited.
In untreated high-risk patients, initiation of parental 
prostacyclin therapy should be considered. These agents 
are the only drug class with proven efficacy in WHO Class 
IV and are expected to exert dose-dependent effects.
102,103 
Common side effects at high doses include headache, 
flushing, jaw and limb pain, nausea, and diarrhea, most of 
which occur early after initiation or dose escalation and 
often improve over time.
104 However, high-dose therapy 
may also trigger complications such as IgG4-related disease, 
thyroid dysfunction, and pituitary disorders. 105 Thus, 
treatment should be administered at experienced centers.
 ▋ 3.5 Activin Signaling Inhibitors
Sotatercept, an activin signaling inhibitor, was approved 
for use and reimbursed in Japan in August 2025. In the 
STELLAR trial,
69 compared with placebo, sotatercept 
significantly improved the 6-minute walk distance (by 
41
 m) at 24 weeks (P<0.001). Because more than half the
 
participants were on background triple therapy, sotatercept 
is expected to be effective as add-on treatment in patients 
requiring further intensification.
106 Therefore, sotatercept 
is considered a promising fourth-line agent that may be 
added to the existing 3 pharmacologic pathways (Figure
 
6). 
However, adverse effects such as bleeding, worsening 
telangiectasia, increased hemoglobin, and decreased platelet 
counts have been reported.
69 Continued accumulation of 
long-term safety data is necessary.
4. PAH Associated With Non-SSc CTDs
Non-SSc CTD-PAH frequently occurs in patients with 
systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE) and mixed connective 
tissue disease. Treatment should follow the general approach 
for I/HPAH, including supportive and vasodilator therapies, 
with immunosuppressive therapy considered at experienced 
centers.
 ▋ 4.1 Diagnosis
PAH and CTD often develop simultaneously. Therefore, 
new-onset PAH warrants screening for underlying CTD. 
Suggestive signs include skin rash, arthritis, cytopenia, 
nephritis (proteinuria, hematuria), serositis (pleural or peri-
cardial effusion), hypergammaglobulinemia, and prolonged 
activated partial thromboplastin time. Laboratory testing 
should include autoantibodies (anti-double-stranded DNA, 
anti-Smith, anti-U1 ribonucleoprotein particle, anti-Ro/
SSA, anti-La/SSB, lupus anticoagulant, anti-cardiolipin, 
anti-β
2 glycoprotein I, Coombs test, antinuclear antibodies) 
and complement levels (C3, C4, CH50). Diagnosis should 
be guided by international classification criteria or Japan’s 
criteria for designated intractable diseases.
Recommendation Table 9. Recommendations and Levels of Evidence for the Treatment of Non-SSc CTD-PAH

disease-associated PAH (non-SSc CTD-PAH) according to the latest guidelines I A
 It is recommended to apply the same treatment algorithm as for IPAH to patients with non-SSc 
CTD-PAH I C

cyclophosphamide in patients with non-SSc CTD-PAH IIa C
 It may be considered to annually assess the risk of PAH in patients with other connective tissue 
diseases that have features of SSc IIb C

Standard treatment for CTD includes immunosup

pression, comprising glucocorticoids and, depending on 
severity, methylprednisolone pulse therapy, immunosup-
pressants, antimalarials, and biologics. PAH is also 
considered an organ manifestation of CTD with presumed 
immunopathogenesis. In fact, the efficacy of immunosup-
pressant therapy, particularly in the early phase, has been 
demonstrated in numerous case reports, series, and retro-
spective studies.
84,107–112
Retrospective studies in Japan suggest that early 
combination therapy with glucocorticoids and immuno-
suppressants significantly improves prognosis in patients 
with the simultaneous onset of PAH and CTD.
111 Predictors 
of a favorable response include WHO Class I/II, preserved 
cardiac index, and early-stage PAH.
108
Two Japanese guidelines already recommend immuno-
suppressant therapy for non-SSc CTD-PAH. Specifically:
•

•   for   SLE-associated   PAH,   early   immunosuppressant 
therapy with glucocorticoids and cyclophosphamide is
 
recommended (evidence level C).114
Although there is no standard immunosuppressive 
regimen, the most commonly reported approach involves 
moderate-to-high doses of glucocorticoids combined with 
cyclophosphamide.
84,107–111 Cyclophosphamide is typically 
administered as intermittent intravenous pulses, 500–
1,000
 mg per dose, once monthly for 3–10 doses.
Hydroxychloroquine, an antimalarial, is effective in
 
preventing SLE flares and may improve long-term 
prognosis.
114 It should be considered in all SLE patients. 
However, because of the risk of retinal toxicity, regular 
ophthalmologic monitoring before and during treatment is 
required.
114–117
5. Portopulmonary Hypertension
 ▋ 5.1 Diagnosis of Portal Hypertension
Direct measurement of portal pressure is invasive; hence, 
determination of the hepatic venous pressure gradient 
(HVPG) is commonly used as an indirect method of 
measurement.
118 HVPG is calculated as the difference 
between wedged hepatic venous pressure and free hepatic 
venous pressure. An HVPG of 1–5
 mmHg is considered
 
normal, HVPG 5–10
 mmHg indicates mild portal hyper
-
tension, and HVPG ≥10
 mmHg is defined as clinically 
significant portal hypertension.118
Non-invasive surrogate markers such as platelet count, 
total bile acids, Mac-2 binding protein glycosylation 
isomer, and autotaxin have been reported to correlate 
with HVPG.
119 Imaging modalities such as abdominal 
ultrasound, contrast-enhanced CT for detecting portosys-
temic shunts, endoscopy for esophageal varices, and liver/
spleen stiffness measured by ultrasound or magnetic 
resonance elastography have also shown correlations with 
HVPG.
119 In Western countries, liver stiffness <15 kPa and  
a platelet count >150,000/μL, as measured by FibroScan, 
are considered sufficient to exclude portal hypertension.118
 ▋ 5.2 Treatment (Recommendation Table  10)
Patients with portopulmonary hypertension (PoPH) should 
be referred to specialized centers capable of managing both 
PH and liver disease. Even in patients with elevated 
pulmonary artery pressure, if they present with high cardiac 
output and normal PVR, thus not fulfilling PH criteria, 
pulmonary vasodilator therapy is not recommended.
  ▋ 5.2.1 General Management of PAH
General treatment measures, such as diuretics, oxygen 
therapy, and exercise limitation, are similar to those for
 
other types of PAH. Because PoPH patients often have high 
cardiac output, enhancing diuretic therapy is recommended 
to prevent pulmonary congestion and right heart failure. 
However, caution is necessary in patients with ascites, 
because intravascular plasma volume may be reduced.
  ▋ 5.2.2 Pulmonary Vasodilator Therapy
Several small studies have reported hemodynamic and 
exercise capacity improvements with sildenafil, bosentan,
 
and ambrisentan in patients with PoPH. Although these 
studies did not stratify patients according to the severity of 
liver dysfunction, most participants were classified as 
Recommendation Table 10. Recommendations and Levels of Evidence for the Treatment of PoPH
COR LOE
 It is recommended to perform echocardiography in patients with liver disease or portal hypertension

screening before liver transplantation
I C

Child-Pugh A or B.120–123
The PORTICO trial, a multicenter double-blind placebo-
controlled randomized controlled trial using macitentan, is 
the only such trial targeting PoPH. Among 85 patients with 
Child-Pugh A or B cirrhosis, those receiving macitentan 
(10
 mg) showed a 35% reduction in PVR compared with
 
placebo (geometric mean ratio 0.65; 95% confidence interval 
0.59–0.72; P<0.0001).
124
The 2022 ESC/ERS guidelines treat PoPH as a distinct 
subgroup with a separate treatment algorithm.7 According 
to the Japanese PAH Registry, 68% of PoPH patients 
received combination therapy, which improved hemody-
namics.
125 Initial treatment should start with monotherapy, 
with stepwise escalation depending on liver disease status 
and eligibility for liver transplantation. Unlike in other PAH 
types, there is little evidence supporting initial combination 
therapy in PoPH.

  ▋ 5.2.3 Role of Liver Transplantation in PoPH
Eligibility for liver transplantation is primarily determined
 
by the severity of liver failure. In PoPH, liver transplan-
tation can be safely performed if mPAP is <35
 mmHg or
 
PVR is <250
 
dyn
 
·
 
s
 
·
 
cm–5. Nonetheless, PH remains a 
significant risk factor for transplant outcomes.126 Therefore, 
echocardiographic screening for PH is recommended prior 
to liver transplantation in patients with portal hypertension. 
Recent reports suggest that even patients with mPAP 
≥35
 mmHg and PVR 
≥3 Wood units may achieve hemody-
namic improvement with preoperative vasodilator therapy, 
allowing for successful transplantation, with a 6-month 
post-transplant survival rate of 76% (19/25 patients).
127
6. PAH Associated With Adult  
Congenital Heart Disease
 ▋ 6.1 Diagnosis
Diagnosis of PAH in adult congenital heart disease (ACHD) 
involves clinical symptoms, history, physical examination, 
imaging (especially echocardiography), and exercise testing. 
A comprehensive understanding of the hemodynamics of 
the underlying congenital heart disease is essential. Definitive 
diagnosis and treatment planning require RHC to calculate 
the pulmonary to systemic blood flow ratio (Qp/Qs), using 
the Fick method when an intracardiac shunt is present. 
Multimodal assessment using CT, magnetic resonance 
imaging, and exercise testing is also important.
 ▋ 6.2 Treatment (Recommendation Tables  11–13)
Outcomes for ACHD-PAH have improved markedly due 
to advances in PAH-targeted therapies, surgical and trans-
catheter interventions, and multidisciplinary care centered 
on ACHD specialists.
128–131 Multidisciplinary support, 
including patient education, rehabilitation, and psychosocial 
services, is essential.
132
Patients with left-to-right shunts and no significant PVR 
elevation may be candidates for surgical or transcatheter 
closure.
133,134 In simple ACHD-PAH, closure recommen-
dations have been based on Qp/Qs and PVR values. 
Traditionally, patients with high PVR were contraindicated 
for closure. However, recent reports from Japan suggest 
that in patients with severe PAH, if PVR improves with 
vasodilators, shunt closure may further benefit PAH.
134–136
This strategy, namely “treat and repair”, is gaining 
clinical traction, particularly in atrial septal defect-related 
PAH, where transcatheter closure without cardiopulmonary 
bypass is possible.
134,136,137 Similar outcomes have been 
reported in ventricular septal defect-related PAH. 135 
Whether to close the defect completely or leave a fenes-
tration remains to be investigated, but this approach may 
improve prognosis and calls for long-term evaluation.

for PAH. Since the 6th World Symposium on Pulmonary

8. HI V-Associated PAH  
(Recommendation Table
 
15)
 ▋ 8.1 Diagnosis
HIV-PAH presents similarly to IPAH. Routine PH screening 
in asymptomatic HIV patients is not recommended. 
However, unexplained dyspnea warrants evaluation with 
BNP/NT-proBNP and echocardiography. HIV-PAH is 
more common in men and in intravenous drug users.
138,139 
Evaluation should include consideration of liver diseases 
(e.g., hepatitis B/C) and exposure to other PAH risk factors. 
Due to advances in antiretroviral therapy and PAH treat-
ments, outcomes for HIV-PAH are improving, although 
early diagnosis remains crucial.
139
 ▋ 8.2 Treatment Strategy
Despite being based on retrospective studies, highly active 
antiretroviral therapy (HAART) improves functional status 
and survival in HIV-PAH, and is recommended regardless 
of viral load or CD4
+ count.7,140,141 When combining PAH 
drugs with protease inhibitors (which inhibit cytochrome 
P450 3A4), blood concentrations may increase, heightening 
the risk of adverse effects. Drug–drug interactions are 
common in HIV treatment; PAH drugs should be initiated 
cautiously and at low doses.
HIV-PAH treatment regimens mirror those used in 
IPAH, with adjustments for interactions with HAART. If 
monotherapy is insufficient based on risk stratification, 
combination therapy should be considered.
7
Among first-generation PAH drugs, bosentan has shown 
efficacy in small short-term open-label trials.142 Long-term 
benefits have also been reported. 143 Sildenafil has been 
reported as effective in a few cases.144,145 Epoprostenol has 
demonstrated significant improvements in WHO functional 
class, exercise capacity, hemodynamics, and survival.
138 
Future studies are needed to confirm the efficacy and safety 
of newer drug combinations in this population.
9. PAH With Features of PVOD/PCH 
(Recommendation Table
 
16)
PVOD and PCH are rare forms of PH due to distal 
pulmonary vascular lesions, often also involving pulmonary 
arteries. They are thus considered part of the PAH spectrum. 
Mutations in EIF2AK4 have been identified in association 
with these conditions.
146–148
Clinical signs suggestive of PVOD/PCH include severe 
hypoxemia, reduced DLCO, and high-resolution CT 
findings such as interlobular septal thickening, centrilobular 
ground-glass opacities/nodules, and mediastinal lymph-
adenopathy.
147–149
Pulmonary vasodilators may cause pulmonary edema in 
these patients, necessitating caution. Although a definitive 
diagnosis requires histology, an early clinical diagnosis is 
essential due to poor prognosis, and lung transplantation 
should be considered.
 ▋ 9.1 Clinical Diagnosis
Given the risks of lung biopsy, diagnosis should be based 
on clinical findings (Table
 
8).150 Key clues include resting 
and exertional desaturation and severely reduced %DLCO 
(<50%) despite normal spirometry.
Chest X-ray may show ground-glass opacities and Kerley 
B lines. High-resolution CT findings include subpleural 
interlobular septal thickening, centrilobular ground-glass 
opacities, and mediastinal lymphadenopathy.
151 Ventilation/
perfusion scans may reveal subsegmental perfusion defects 
(especially in upper lobes). RHC shows precapillary PH

10. Lung Transplantation
 ▋ 10.1 General Indications
The Japanese criteria for lung and heart–lung transplant 
recipients were first published in 1997 and updated in 2015. 
In 2023, HIV antibody positivity was removed as an 
exclusion criterion due to advances in HIV treatment.
157 
The current criteria include general indications (Table
 
9),157 
eligible diseases, and exclusion criteria (Table
 
10).157 Trans-
plantation is indicated for patients with progressive lung 
disease unresponsive to optimal medical therapy and with 
no other life-saving options. Generally, candidates should 
be aged under 55 years for bilateral lung transplants and 
under 60 years for single-lung transplants.
In Japan, the average wait time for brain-dead lung trans-
plantation is long: 525 days for actively listed patients and 
1,008 days when including temporarily inactive patients.
158 
Timely listing is critical. As of the end of 2022, 1,036 lung 
transplants had been performed in Japan (excluding heart–
lung transplants), of which 158 (15.3%) were for PAH 
(mostly bilateral).
159
 ▋ 10.2 Indications in PAH
The International Society for Heart and Lung Transplan-
tation published guidelines in 1998 and revised them in 
2006, 2014,
160 and 2021.161 Each version of these guidelines 
defines the timing for referral to transplant centers and 
listing on transplant waitlists. The 2021 update of the 
guidelines reflects recent advances in PAH risk stratification. 
Two widely used models are REVEAL 2.0
162 and the 2015 
ESC/ERS model.163

IV. Group 2 Pulmonary Hypertension Due to Left Heart Disease
PM?c
Pulmonary hypertension (PH) caused by elevated pulmo-
nary venous pressure due to left heart disease (LHD) is 
classified as Group 2 PH (PH-LHD), which is the most 
common subtype of PH.
7 PH-LHD is further categorized 
according to the underlying condition as isolated post-
capillary PH (IpcPH) or combined post- and precapillary 
PH (CpcPH), defined as follows:
• 
 IpcPH:
  mean
  pulmonary
  arterial
  pressure
  (mPAP) 
>20
 mmHg, pulmonary arterial wedge pressure (PAWP)
 
>15
 mmHg, and 
pulmonary vascular resistance (PVR) 
≤2 Wood units
•   C
pcPH: m
PAP >
20
 m
mHg, P
AWP
 >
15
 m
mHg, a
nd P
VR 
>2 Wood units.
Previously, the 
diastolic pressure gradient (DPG; 
calculated as dPAP minus PAWP) was used to differentiate 
between IpcPH and CpcPH (DPG <7 for IpcPH DPG ≥7 
for CpcPH), but it is no longer used because it is not 
considered predictive of prognosis.
164
2. Diagnosis
In heart failure with preserved ejection fraction (HFpEF), 
PAWP may fall below 15
 mmHg with treatment, and
 
persistent PH may be misclassified as precapillary PH. 
Therefore, caution is necessary. Scoring systems such as 
HFA-PEFF and H
2FPEF may help identify HFpEF as the 
underlying cause of PH. 165–167 If LHD is suspected 
(Table
 
11)164 and PH is moderate to severe and differentiation 
from chronic thromboembolic pulmonary hypertension or 
pulmonary arterial hypertension is required, or if advanced 
heart failure warrants transplant evaluation, right heart 
catheterization should be considered.
164 Accurate hemody-
namic assessment requires optimal treatment of the under-
lying LHD and a stable condition prior to right heart 
catheterization.
7
3. Treatment
 ▋ 3.1  Treatment of Underlying Disease 
(Recommendation Table
 
17)
To correct PH-LHD, the most important step is to treat 
the underlying LHD appropriately to reduce pulmonary 
venous pressure. Therefore, in LHD patients in whom PH 
is suspected, optimization of the underlying disease should 
precede PH evaluation. If left ventricular dysfunction is 
suspected, heart failure treatment should follow the 2025 
Revised Heart Failure Management Guidelines.
168
 ▋ 3.2  Pulmonary Vasodilator Therapy for PH-LHD 
(Recommendation Table
 
18)
Several trials have evaluated the efficacy of pulmonary 
vasodilators in heart failure with reduced ejection fraction 
(HFrEF). Although prostacyclin and endothelin pathway 
agents have shown improvement in hemodynamics, New 
York Heart Association functional class, and the 6-minute 
walk distance, no survival benefit has been demon-
strated.
169,170 Evidence on phosphodiesterase 5 inhibitors is 
mixed.171 In the LEPHT trial of riociguat, modest improve-
ments were observed in hemodynamics and activities of 
daily living, but the primary endpoint (change in mPAP) 
was not significantly improved.
172 The BADDHY trial 
evaluating bosentan in HFpEF with PH found no benefit 
in exercise tolerance.
173
In the MELODY-1 trial targeting CpcPH (with 73% 
of patients having HFpEF), macitentan increased fluid 
retention.
174 Across all trials of endothelin receptor antag-

onists, early adverse events, such as fluid retention, have 
been reported, and there is no evidence supporting their 
efficacy.
In the RELAX trial of sildenafil, no improvement in the 
primary outcome (exercise tolerance) was observed.
175 
However, in studies focused on CpcPH patients, sildenafil 
improved 6-minute walk distance, pulmonary hemody-
namics, and right ventricular function.
176 In the study of 
Guazzi et al., patients with high PVR and impaired right 
ventricular function showed improvements in PAWP, 
PVR, and right ventricular function.
177 The DYNAMIC 
trial of riociguat showed improvement in cardiac output at 
26 weeks, but not in exercise capacity or quality of life.
178
The REDUCE LAP-HF II trial evaluating atrial shunt 
devices in HFpEF showed no overall efficacy.179 However, 
improvements in PVR, pulmonary artery elastance, and 
compliance were observed in a subset of patients.
Pulmonary artery denervation has shown benefit in 
PAH
180 and one report suggested that, in CpcPH, pulmo-
nary artery denervation led to improved PVR and exercise 
tolerance compared with sildenafil.181
In PH due to valvular heart disease, surgical or trans-
catheter valve repair is the first-line treatment and leads to 
hemodynamic improvement.
182 However, persistent PH 
after valve correction is associated with poor prognosis.183,184 
Functional mitral regurgitation occurs in both HFrEF and 
HFpEF. According to the 2020 Revised Valvular Heart 
Disease Treatment Guidelines,
185 intervention for severe 
mitral regurgitation associated with heart failure symptoms 
is recommended.
182 In aortic stenosis, preoperative PH is 
associated with worse long-term outcomes. 186–191 Even 
moderate postoperative PH correlates with poor outcomes. 
In these patients, sildenafil worsened outcomes compared 
with placebo.
186–192
In summary, aggressive use of pulmonary vasodilators 
is not recommended for PH-LHD. However, in LHD 
patients with a significant precapillary component (generally 
PVR >5 Wood units), individualized therapy may be 
considered at the discretion of specialists. Pulmonary 
vasodilators should be avoided in IpcPH.

V. Group 3 Pulmonary Hypertension Associated With Lung Diseases and/or Hypoxia
PM?c?`??c???
Severe Pulmonary Hypertension in  
Group 3 Pulmonary Hypertension
In Group 3 pulmonary hypertension (PH), a pulmonary 
vascular phenotype is recognized, characterized by mild 
obstructive or restrictive ventilatory impairment and limited 
parenchymal abnormalities on chest computed tomography, 
with a predominant pulmonary vascular component. The 
distinction between this phenotype and pulmonary arterial 
hypertension (PAH) with coexisting lung disease is often 
unclear.
193–195 Clinical features and prognosis are reported 
to be similar between Group 3 PH treated with vasodilators 
and PAH with lung disease.
89
Group 3 PH and PAH can be differentiated by the extent 
of ventilatory impairment (forced vital capacity <70% or 
forced expiratory volume in 1 second <60%) and paren-
chymal abnormalities. In Group 3 PH, exercise limitation 
primarily results from ventilatory dysfunction, and the 
reduction in the diffusing capacity of the lungs for carbon 
monoxide (DLCO) parallels the degree of ventilatory 
impairment, whereas in PAH, DLCO is disproportionately 
low, reflecting circulatory limitation.
194
Echocardiography is the initial screening tool, but it is 
often infeasible or inaccurate in advanced lung disease.196,197 
Right heart catheterization (RHC) is essential for definitive 
diagnosis and is recommended when lung transplantation 
or lung volume reduction surgery is considered, when PAH 
or chronic thromboembolic pulmonary hypertension is 
suspected, or when additional hemodynamic data are 
needed for phenotyping or treatment planning.
7,194,198 
Hemodynamic assessment should be performed when a 
patient is clinically stable, because severe PH may appear 
transiently during exacerbations due to hypoxemia.
7,199 
Traditionally, severe Group 3 PH has been defined as a 
mean pulmonary arterial pressure (mPAP) ≥35
 
mmHg, or 
o`o?≥QT
 mmHg with a low cardiac index (<2.5 or
 
<2
 
L/min/m2).194,200 Recent registry data in chronic 
obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD) and interstitial 
lung disease (ILD) indicate that pulmonary vascular 
resistance (PVR) >5 Wood units predicts poor prognosis 
regardless of mPAP, and this threshold is now recommended 
in the European Society of Cardiology/European Respiratory 
Society guidelines.
7,199,201
2. PH Associated With ILD
 ▋ 2.1 Diagnosis
When worsening dyspnea, decreased SpO 2, or reduced 
exercise tolerance are observed without clear progression 
of ILD, the coexistence of PH should be suspected.
202 
RHC is necessary for diagnosing PH, although echocar-
diography is often used for screening. However, in a study 
of 374 patients with severe lung disease awaiting lung 
transplantation, 48% of patients were misdiagnosed with 
PH using echocardiography. Discrepancies were also noted 
between estimated right ventricular pressures and those 
measured by RHC.
196
Combining B-type natriuretic peptide, DLCO, and 
echocardiography for screening has been reported to 
improve the diagnostic accuracy for ILD-PH and may help 
identify patients with ILD who require RHC.
203
 ▋ 2.2 Treatment
  ▋ 2.2.1 Treatment of ILD
Treatment 
for ILD includes corticosteroids and immuno-
suppressive agents. More recently, antifibrotic drugs have 
been used for idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis (IPF) and 
progressive fibrosing ILD.
204 In addition to pharmacologic 
therapy, appropriate pulmonary rehabilitation and initia-
tion of long-term oxygen therapy (LTOT) are important. 
When PH is present in ILD with chronic respiratory failure, 
LTOT is often initiated due to suspected hypoxic pulmonary 
vasoconstriction. However, there is no conclusive evidence 
that LTOT improves prognosis. If PH persists despite 
adequate ILD treatment, pulmonary vasodilator therapy 
may be considered depending on the severity of PH.
  ▋ 2.2.2 Systemic Pulmonary Vasodilator Therapy
Several studies have evaluated pulmonary vasodilators
 
for ILD-PH, with some small studies showing potential 
improvements in pulmonary hemodynamics.
205,206 However, 
a randomized controlled trial of bosentan in idiopathic 
interstitial pneumonia-associated PH showed no improve-
ment in PVR, World Health Organization (WHO) functional 
class, or symptoms.
207 In the COMPERA registry, phospho-
diesterase 5 (PDE5) inhibitors also did not improve exercise 
tolerance.
208 Nevertheless, in treatment responders, defined 
as those with post-treatment improvement in PVR or 
WHO functional class, survival was better, especially in 
connective tissue disease ILD-PH, suggesting possible 
responsiveness in this subgroup.
200,208,209 PDE5 inhibitors 
are often used in ILD-PH because they cause less venti-
lation–perfusion mismatch.
210 However, supporting evidence 
for improvements in prognosis or clinical parameters 
remains limited.
211,212 Combination with antifibrotic agents 
has not demonstrated significant benefits in terms of quality 
of life (QOL) or disease progression.
213,214
Ambrisentan and riociguat are not recommended for 
IPF or idiopathic interstitial pneumonia-associated PH due 
to findings of serious adverse events, increased mortality, 
and lack of clinical benefit.
215–217 Overall, the effectiveness 
of pulmonary vasodilators for ILD-PH remains unclear, 
and a meta-analysis showed no superiority in terms of 
6-minute walk distance (6MWD), respiratory function, or 
severe adverse events.
218
  ▋ 2.2.3 Inhaled Treprostinil for ILD-PH
In 2021, the results of a randomized controlled trial
 
evaluating inhaled treprostinil for ILD-PH were published.219 
Treprostinil resulted in a significant improvement in the 
primary endpoint (i.e., 6MWD after 16 weeks) of 31.12
 m
 
(95% confidence interval 16.85–45.39
 m; P<0.001). In
 
addition, a reduction in clinical worsening was observed in 
the treprostinil arm. Inhaled treprostinil was particularly 
effective in patients with severe disease (baseline 6MWD 
≤350
 m, %DLCO <40%, or PVR 
≥4 Wood units).
Furthermore, inhaled treprostinil has been reported to 
slow the decline in forced vital capacity in ILD-PH, 
suggesting it may be beneficial as a treatment for the 
Advance Publication
[[p29]]
29
JCS/JPCPHS 2025 Guideline on PH and VTE
underlying ILD.220
In Japan, as of September 2024, the indication for 
inhaled treprostinil was expanded to include ILD-PH. 
Further investigations are needed to determine the long-
term efficacy of inhaled treprostinil and its impact on 
prognosis.

Several studies have examined the impact of PH severity 
on the effectiveness of pulmonary vasodilators in ILD-PH. 
These studies include reports showing acute hemodynamic 
improvement with sildenafil in IPF-PH,

3. PH Associated With COPD and Emphysema 
(Recommendation Table
 
19)
 ▋ 3.1 Diagnosis
Echocardiography is typically the first imaging modality 
used for non-invasive diagnosis of COPD-PH. However, 
in advanced COPD, hyperinflated lungs often hinder 
adequate visualization, reducing diagnostic accuracy.
196,197 
In severe COPD-PH, plasma BNP or N-terminal pro 
B-type natriuretic peptide levels may be elevated, but 
sensitivity is low for detecting moderate disease.
222 RHC is 
considered the gold standard for diagnosing COPD-PH 
and is recommended when its findings would significantly 
impact therapeutic decision-making.
7,194,198
 ▋ 3.2 Treatment
COPD management, including treatment of the underlying 
disease, oxygen therapy, and respiratory rehabilitation, 
should follow current guidelines.
194,223
 ▋ 3.3 Long-term Oxygen Therapy
According to the Nocturnal Oxygen Therapy Trial group 
in the US, in patients with COPD and PaO
2 ≤55  mmHg or 
PaO2 ≤59  mmHg with edema, cor pulmonale, or polycy-
themia, 24-h oxygen therapy significantly improved prog-
nosis and PVR compared with nighttime-only oxygen 
therapy.
224
Similarly, the UK Medical Research Council (MRC) 
working party reported that more than 15
 h of oxygen
 
therapy improved prognosis compared with no oxygen 
therapy in COPD patients with PaO
2 40–60 mmHg and  
accompanying hypercapnia or heart failure.225 The use of 
LTOT for 15
 h 
daily suppressed mPAP elevation, and 
extending therapy to over 18
 h slightly reduced mPAP.226
However, for COPD patients with resting SpO 2 of 
89–93%, 24-h LTOT over 1–6 years did not improve 
mortality or delay hospitalization (as shown by the LTOT 
Research Group).
227 Thus, patients without resting PaO 2 
≤60
 mmHg may derive limited benefit from LTOT.
 
Nonetheless, even when PaO2 is high, those with elevated 
PAP or a low cardiac index may have reduced PvO 2 (a 
marker of tissue oxygenation), which should be taken into 
consideration.
228
 ▋ 3.4 Pulmonary Vasodilators
The indication for PAH-specific drugs in COPD-PH is not 
established. In severe COPD-PH (mPAP 39
 mmHg, PVR 7
 
Wood units), sildenafil was reported to improve pulmonary 
hemodynamics, the BODE index, and QOL measures (e.g., 
Modified MRC and 36-Item Short Form Health Survey 
[SF-36]).
229 However, in mild COPD-PH (mPAP 32 mmH g, 
PVR 4.2 Wood units), it did not improve 6MWD or 
hemodynamics.
230
In the PERFECT trial involving 136 COPD-PH patients 
(mPAP 42
 mmHg, 
PVR 7.2 Wood units), inhaled treprostinil 
did not improve the primary endpoint (6MWD), and 
fatigue, dyspnea, and serious adverse events were more 
common in the treatment group, leading to early trial 
termination.
231 Inhaled treprostinil is not recommended for 
COPD-PH. Meta-analyses to date have shown that 
although pulmonary vasodilators improve hemodynamics, 
they offer limited benefit in 6MWD.
223,232–237
Caution is needed due to the risk that relieving hypoxic 
vasoconstriction with vasodilators may worsen ventilation–
perfusion mismatch.
223 Registries show that approximately 
30% of patients with severe PH exhibit an improvement in 
functional class or PVR, and responders have improved 
survival.
223,238
For patients with suspected severe COPD-PH or when 
there is uncertainty in management, consultation with 
specialists in lung disease and PH (or referral to an expert 
center) is recommended.
7,194,223

VI. Group 4 Chronic Thromboembolic Pulmonary Hypertension
PM?c??s?`K??
Chronic Thromboembolic Pulmonary  
Hypertension Team
 ▋ 1.1 Diagnostic Algorithm
Chronic thromboembolic pulmonary hypertension (CTEPH) 
is a condition that should be suspected in all patients with 
pulmonary hypertension (PH) at the time of diagnosis, and 
tests should be conducted to confirm or rule out the disease. 
Risk factors for CTEPH
239 include a history of acute 
pulmonary embolism (PE), intravenous devices (pacemaker, 
long-term central venous line, ventriculoatrial shunt), 
inflammatory bowel disease, essential thrombocythemia, 
polycythemia vera, splenectomy, antiphospholipid syndrome, 
high-dose thyroid hormone replacement therapy, and 
cancer. In patients with a history of acute PE, CTEPH 
should be suspected if: at the time of PE diagnosis, computed 
tomography pulmonary angiography (CTPA) shows orga-
nized mural thrombi with a morphology clearly different 
from acute thrombi;
240 after 3 months of adequate antico-
agulation therapy, echocardiography findings remain highly 
suggestive of PH;
7 or the patient experiences persistent 
dyspnea and exercise limitation during the clinical course241 
(Figure
 
8;7 Table  122,242,243). In the 2022 European Society 
of Cardiology/European Resiratory Society guidelines, 7 
the CTEPH diagnostic algorithm mandates that patients

suspected of having PH on echocardiography undergo 
blood tests, electrocardiography, chest X-ray, arterial blood 
gas analysis, pulmonary function tests, and high-resolution 
chest computed tomography (CT) to exclude lung disease 
and left heart disease. After these are excluded, a ventilation–
perfusion (V/Q) scan should be performed to exclude 
CTEPH. V/Q scans are highly sensitive and specific for 
detecting CTEPH and are particularly useful tests. However, 
because of issues such as isotope shortages and the need 
for large, specialized equipment, V/Q scans cannot be 
performed at every institution. In recent years, there have 
been reports on diagnostic modalities for CTEPH-related 
perfusion defects using iodine mapping CT, dual-energy 
CT, magnetic resonance imaging, cone-beam CT, and 
analysis for dynamic chest radiography. However, as of 
March 2025, no prospective study has been performed, 
and these modalities have not yet been established. If a 
V/Q scan indicates suspicion of CTEPH, confirming PH by 
right heart catheterization and demonstrating organized 
thrombi in the pulmonary arteries by pulmonary angiog-
raphy or CTPA establishes a diagnosis of CTEPH. 
Differential diagnoses of CTEPH include pulmonary 
artery sarcoma or malignant tumors (e.g., renal carcinoma, 
uterine carcinoma, metastatic germ cell tumors), arteritis, 
peripheral pulmonic stenosis, parasitic infection (hydatid 
cyst), and foreign body embolism, all of which can cause 
pulmonary artery narrowing or occlusion. In addition, 
there is a condition of chronic thromboembolic pulmonary 
disease (CTEPD) without PH, in which unresolved 
pulmonary clots lead to residual perfusion defects without 
meeting the hemodynamic definition of PH. Especially in 
symptomatic patients, CTEPD without PH should be 
diagnosed and treated similarly to CTEPH. (See Chapter 
1, Section 6 on transition to chronic conditions [post-
thrombotic syndrome and CTEPD].)
 ▋ 1.2 CTEPH Treatment Algorithm
Until around 2010, pulmonary endarterectomy (PEA) 
was the treatment of choice for CTEPH, and treatment 
algorithms primarily focused on determining surgical oper-
ability. PEA performed under deep hypothermic circulatory 
arrest can potentially normalize pulmonary hemodynamics 
(~65% reduction in pulmonary vascular resistance [PVR]) 
and restore exercise tolerance. However, in the past decade, 
balloon pulmonary angioplasty (BPA) and medical 
therapies have advanced, dramatically changing CTEPH 
treatment. Therefore, a multimodal treatment approach 
needs to be considered.
The treatment algorithm requires a multidisciplinary 
approach that combines PEA, BPA, and a pulmonary 
vasodilator, targeting proximal, peripheral, and micro-
vascular disease components, respectively. The latest 
CTEPH treatment algorithm (Figure
 
9) reflects this compre-
hensive approach. All CTEPH patients should receive 
lifelong anticoagulation. Historically, vitamin K antagonists 
were used, but in recent years evidence has been reported 
supporting the use of direct oral anticoagulants (DOACs) 
as an alternative.
244–248 In a randomized controlled trial 
(RCT) comparing continued warfarin vs. switching to 
edoxaban, edoxaban was non-inferior in terms of change 
in PVR at 1 year.
244 Anticoagulation is essential to prevent 
recurrent thrombosis and should be continued lifelong, 
even after PH improves following any intervention (e.g., 
PEA, BPA). In addition, general management for CTEPH 
includes considering oxygen therapy in the case of 
hypoxemia and using diuretics or inotropic agents if right 
heart failure is present.
Ideally, patients with CTEPH should be managed at 
specialized CTEPH/BPA centers by a multidisciplinary 
team. The CTEPH team ideally includes an experienced 
PEA surgeon, PH specialist, BPA interventionist, and a 
Ongoing follow-up by an MDT at a specialized CTEPH/BPA center (Class I)
