radiologist trained in CTEPH imaging, although not all 
centers can staff every specialty. However, especially for 
assessment of surgical candidacy, it is recommended to 
consult with experienced centers, either in person or online, 
with referral as appropriate.
In formulating a treatment strategy, mechanical 
interventions targeting the organized thrombi should be 
considered first. Thus, whether the lesions are surgically 
accessible should be evaluated first. If organized thrombi 
are located in proximal pulmonary arteries, the patient is 
considered suitable for PEA. If the lesions are peripheral 
(organized thrombi confined to segmental and subsegmental 
branches), the disease is considered more suitable for BPA. 
Surgical candidacy is determined based on lesion location 
and the patient’s condition, including age and any comor-
bidities.
249,250 If the patient is deemed a surgical candidate, 
PEA is recommended, but BPA may be considered after 
weighing the risks and benefits. In the case of mixed 
lesions, where one lung has surgically accessible proximal 
disease and the other has peripheral disease, BPA alone or 
a hybrid approach of combined BPA and PEA can be 
considered. To reduce the risks of PEA, performing BPA 
or administering pulmonary vasodilators beforehand can 
also be considered. For patients who are not candidates for 
PEA, a combination of pulmonary vasodilator therapy 
(riociguat, selexipag) and BPA is recommended. Notably, 
there is evidence that starting a pulmonary vasodilator 
before BPA may reduce BPA-related serious adverse events; 
therefore, in patients with PVR ≥4 Wood units, initiating 
medical therapy prior to BPA should be considered.
251 
Studies have found that if, after PEA, a patient’s mean 
pulmonary arterial pressure (mPAP) is ≥ 38
 
mmHg and 
PVR is ≥ 5 Wood units, long-term survival is worse. 
Therefore, in patients with residual or recurrent PH and 
persistent symptoms after PEA, a combination of BPA 
and pulmonary vasodilator therapy (riociguat, selexipag) 
is recommended.
249,252,253 If a patient’s condition does not 
improve even with such a multimodal approach, adding 
other pulmonary vasodilators (besides riociguat and 
selexipag) can be considered.
CTEPD without PH also requires lifelong anticoagulation 
therapy. Because pulmonary vasodilators are not approved 
for CTEPD without PH, patients with symptomatic 
CTEPD without PH should be considered for PEA or 
BPA.
254,255
2. BPA (Recommendation Table  20)
 ▋ 2.1 Indications for BPA
Because the efficacy and safety of BPA have been estab-
lished, BPA should be considered for all CTEPH patients 
who have symptoms and are not candidates for PEA. BPA 
can be considered regardless of symptom severity; even 
patients who are asymptomatic at rest but have symptoms 
with exercise may be candidates for BPA.
7 CTEPH patients 
who are not eligible for PEA include: (i) those whose 
disease consists of lesions in locations that are surgically 
inaccessible; and (ii) those with poor surgical tolerance due 
to factors like advanced age or frailty, as well as patients 
with residual PH after PEA in whom repeat surgery is 
deemed difficult. Reports from Japan have shown that 
BPA is effective and safe even in elderly
256,257 and frail 258 
CTEPH patients. In the current guidelines, both categories 
(i) and (ii) described above are equally recommended 
indications for BPA. Moreover, even in patients who have 
technically accessible lesions for surgery but who are 
judged to have inadequate surgical tolerance (due to age, 
frailty, or comorbidities), the efficacy and safety of BPA 
has been shown to be comparable to that in patients with 
distal lesions.
259 Therefore, in deciding between BPA and 
PEA, in addition to the anatomical surgical accessibility of 
thrombi, a patient’s age and overall condition should be 
taken into consideration.
It should be noted that in order to safely handle 
potentially life-threatening complications of BPA, earlier 
statements in 2014 specified detailed criteria for operators 
and institutions performing BPA. However, now that the 
safety of BPA is established when it is performed by 
operators with knowledge of its complications and 
countermeasures, these strict requirements have become 
less critical. Reasonable criteria for defining a BPA center 
are the presence of a Japanese Circulation Society–certified 
BPA operator or instructor and the availability of a multi-
disciplinary CTEPH team with a collaborative relationship 
with an experienced PEA center.
 ▋ 2.2 Imaging and Selective Pulmonary Angiography
Selective pulmonary angiography can reveal lesions such 
as webs, bands, pouches, irregular vessel walls, and chronic 
total occlusions (see Chapter 5, Section 4.12). Based on 
the appearance of lesions on pulmonary angiography, 
Kawakami et al.
260 categorized BPA target lesions into 5 
types: Type A, ring-like stenotic lesions; Type B, webs; 
Type C, subtotal occlusion; Type D, total occlusion; and 
Type E, tortuous lesions. Using this lesion classification, 
the success rate of BPA was high for Type A and B lesions 
(98.6–100%), whereas Type D lesions had the lowest success 
rate (∼ 52%). Complications were reported to be more 
frequent with Type C and E lesions. These findings support 
initiating BPA on lesions with a lower complication risk.
Moreover, findings from selective pulmonary angiog-
raphy can be used to evaluate the effectiveness of BPA 
treatment. A pulmonary flow grade has been proposed to 
assess perfusion from the pulmonary arteries through to 
the pulmonary veins on angiograms: Grade 0, a lesion 
where the pulmonary artery does not opacify; Grade 1, the 
pulmonary artery is visible but blood flow distal to the 
stenosis is slow; Grade 2, the pulmonary artery is opacified 
but only part of the pulmonary veins are opacified; and 
Grade 3, both the pulmonary artery and pulmonary veins 
are opacified.
261 If BPA achieves Grade 3 perfusion, it is 
considered to have yielded the maximum effect. Inami et al. 
reported achieving Grade 3 perfusion in 69.7% of treated 
lesions.
261 Other imaging modalities are also considered 
useful, including optical coherence tomography,262 optical 
frequency domain imaging,263 intravascular ultrasound,264 
and magnetic resonance imaging. However, caution is 
required with optical coherence tomography because the 
pressure injection of contrast into the distal pulmonary 
arteries can cause vessel injury.
265
 ▋ 2.3 BPA Procedure
The pulmonary artery tree is divided into 19 segmental 
branches on each side, and when including subsegmental 
branches, a considerable number of lesions can be present. 
For this reason, BPA treatment is usually completed over 
multiple sessions. The number of sessions needed varies 
Advance Publication
[[p34]]
34
TAMURA Y et al.
among patients, but is approximately 4–5.265–270
  ▋ 2.3.1 Selection of Target Lesions
In PEA, organized thrombi are removed together with a
 
layer of intima as a single piece, so the morphology of 
individual lesions has little influence on treatment candidacy 
or outcomes. In BPA, the morphology of each lesion is 
related to treatment feasibility, efficacy, and complication 
risk.
260,271 Various nomenclatures exist for lesion morphology, 
but the classification proposed by Kawakami et al. 260 is 
widely recognized (Figure
 
10). In the initial BPA session, 
especially when pulmonary artery pressure is high, treatment 
is primarily directed at lesions such as webs or ring-like 
stenoses. After pulmonary pressure has decreased following

initial treatments, lesions that carry relatively higher risk 
can be addressed in later sessions. In recent years, evidence 
has shown that treating all lesions, including subtotal and 
total occlusion lesions, to achieve complete revascular-
ization leads to better improvement in hemodynamics and 
symptoms, suggesting that any lesion can be considered a 
treatment target.
272,273 Accordingly, devices and techniques 
from coronary and peripheral vascular interventions have 
increasingly been applied to BPA.
274–277 It should be noted 
that tortuous lesions have the highest complication rates, 
and therefore require utmost caution during BPA.
  ▋ 2.3.2  Incidence and Management of Intraoperative  
Complications
The incidence of complications with BPA, including minor 
complications, is higher than for coronary interventions. 
Most intraoperative complications are thought to be 
pulmonary hemorrhage resulting from guidewire perfo-
ration or balloon overdilation. Occasionally, the tip of the 
guiding catheter may dissect the pulmonary artery, but the 
incidence of pulmonary artery dissection is low and, in most 
cases, it heals spontaneously without specific treatment.
260,267
Pulmonary hemorrhage that becomes clinically evident 
(such as presenting with hemoptysis) occurs in 5–15% of 
BPA sessions. However, small bleeding events that are not 
clinically apparent but can be detected on post-procedural 
chest X-ray or CT occur at even higher rates.
260,267,269,271,278 
The occurrence of procedure-related pulmonary hemorrhage 
is influenced by factors such as lesion morphology, the tip 
load of the guidewire, and the operator’s skill. Whether a 
bleed manifests clinically (e.g., as hemoptysis) also depends 
on the pulmonary artery pressure and the perfusion 
pressure beyond the dilated lesion.
270,275,279 Therefore, when 
pulmonary artery pressure is high, it is advisable to avoid 
high-risk lesions or techniques and to focus treatment on 
webs, ring-like stenoses, and similar lower-risk lesions.
260,271 
It has also been shown that performing BPA in the setting 
of remarkably high pulmonary artery pressure increases 
the risk of reperfusion pulmonary edema, underscoring the 
importance of choosing an appropriate treatment strategy 
based on the patient’s condition. Measures that have been 
implemented include using a pressure wire to measure the 
mean distal pressure and ensuring that after dilation it 
does not exceed 35
 mmHg, as well as adopting a staged
 
BPA approach starting with small balloons. 270,275,280 In 
addition, administering oxygen or inhaled nitric oxide 
during BPA to lower pulmonary artery pressure may help 
reduce complications.
281,282 In the RACE cross-over trial 
(which compared riociguat to BPA and cross-over in an 
extended period), patients pretreated with riociguat had a 
lower incidence of serious complications during BPA.
251 
To prevent complications like pulmonary hemorrhage 
from becoming life-threatening, early recognition and 
management are crucial. Bleeding is sometimes difficult to 
visualize on angiography, so it is essential to continuously 
monitor not only angiographic signs (e.g., the guidewire’s 
position) but also the patient’s symptoms (e.g., coughing) 
during the procedure. If pulmonary hemorrhage occurs, 
A: ring-like stenosis lesion B: web lesion C: subtotal lesion
D: total occlusion lesion E: tortuous lesion
Figure 10.  Characteristic morphologies of pulmonary artery lesions targeted for balloon pulmonary angioplasty treatment: ( A) 
ring-like stenotic lesion; (B) web lesion; (C) subtotal occlusion; (D) total occlusion; and (E) tortuous lesion.
Advance Publication
[[p36]]
36
TAMURA Y et al.
the key is to promptly stop the bleeding: wedge the catheter 
into the involved branch or inflate a balloon in the proximal 
vessel to occlude blood flow and achieve hemostasis. In 
most cases, these maneuvers will control the bleeding. If 
vessel injury is severe and the bleeding cannot be stopped 
with these methods, embolization with a gelatin sponge or 
coils is effective.
  ▋ 2.3.3 Determining Endpoints
Because BPA treatment is completed over multiple sessions,
 
it is necessary to consider both the overall treatment 
endpoint for the patient and the endpoint for each session. 
The patient-level endpoint is influenced by many factors, 
including hemodynamics (mPAP, PVR), symptoms, age, 
risk of complications, renal function, the patient’s 
preferences, and the patient’s access to a BPA center. In the 
absence of specific contraindications, complete revascular-
ization (i.e., complete dilatation of all treatable pulmonary 
artery lesions) leads to greater improvements in postop-
erative World Health Organization (WHO) functional 
class, oxygenation, exercise tolerance, and other outcomes.
273 
The endpoint for each BPA session should be determined 
by considering both patient-related factors (lesion 
morphology, PH severity, renal function) and non-patient 
factors (radiation exposure, contrast volume, procedure 
time). Unlike coronary intervention, BPA involves the 
treatment of many branches, so there is no absolute criterion 
for when to end a session. Prolonged procedures beyond 
the operator’s concentration may increase the risk of 
complications. Thus, centers may consider establishing 
their own rules for ending a session, such as a maximum 
fluoroscopy time, radiation dose, or contrast volume. In 
addition, as noted above, if a large number of pulmonary 
segments were dilated to their maximum size while baseline 
mPAP remains high, the risk of serious complications can 
increase. In an initial BPA session for a patient with 
extremely high mPAP, a promising strategy is to limit 
treatment to only a few lesions (e.g., a few webs or ring-like 
stenoses in lower lobe regions with high flow) and to use a 
balloon size approximately 50% of the target vessel’s 
diameter, rather than dilating to full size in that first session.
 ▋ 2.4 Pre- and Postoperative Management
  ▋ 2.4.1 Preoperative Management
a.
 Anticoagulation Therapy
A survey of anticoagulation practices in Japan revealed
 
diverse approaches: some centers continue oral anticoagu-
lation up to the BPA session and concurrently administer 
intravenous heparin during the procedure, whereas others 
switch from oral anticoagulants to heparin infusion prior 
to the procedure. There is not yet a standardized antico-
agulation protocol for the peri-BPA period. Nonetheless, 
all reports indicate that some form of anticoagulation is 
maintained during the BPA procedure. Because bleeding 
complications related to the procedure (mainly pulmonary 
vascular injuries) are the most common intraoperative 
complications,
271,282 each institution should prepare protocols 
in advance for how to handle bleeding complications during 
BPA, tailored to their own setting.
b.
 Pulmonary Vasodilators and Inotropic Agents
The most common complication after BPA is lung injury
 
(reperfusion pulmonary edema), which is caused by 
procedure-related factors (primarily guidewire perforation 
or balloon-induced vessel trauma). 271,279,283 However, the 
progression to severe respiratory/circulatory failure due to 
bleeding is associated with the severity of the patient’s 
hemodynamic status at the time of BPA.
261,279,284 Therefore, 
improving hemodynamics with medical therapy before the 
BPA may help prevent BPA-related severe respiratory 
failure. In a subanalysis of the RACE trial, patients who 
received riociguat before BPA had fewer complications 
during BPA than those who underwent BPA alone.
251 The 
2022 European Society of Cardiology (ESC)/European 
Respiratory Society (ERS) guidelines give a Class IIa 
recommendation to initiate riociguat prior to BPA.
7 In 
addition, because the severity of hemodynamic impairment 
correlates with the severity of post-BPA complications, 
dobutamine infusion is empirically administered in patients 
with a low cardiac output,
266,284 and the protocol of the 
Japanese MR BPA study also incorporated this approach.285
  ▋ 2.4.2 Intraoperative Management
As discussed in 
Section 3.3.4,271,279 lung injury is the most 
frequent perioperative complication and arises from 
procedure-related causes. Completely eliminating procedure-
related complications is challenging, but if overt signs such 
as hemoptysis or blood sputum appear during the 
procedure, it is critical to achieve hemostasis before finishing 
the session. Leaving the catheterization laboratory without 
confirming hemostasis can lead to worsening respiratory 
failure due to ongoing hemorrhage, and thus must be 
avoided.
  ▋ 2.4.3 Postoperative Management
a.
 Lung Injury
Lung injury is the leading cause of respiratory failure after
 
BPA. In a Japanese registry published in 2017, 267 lung 
injury was observed in 17.8% of 308 patients, with 
mechanical ventilation required in 5.5% and extracorporeal 
membrane oxygenation required in 2.9%. Hemoptysis 
occurred in 14.0% of patients, which is higher than the 
incidence of evident lung injury on chest X-ray, suggesting 
that some bleeding events occur without overt lung 
opacities. Consequently, the reported incidence of lung 
injury varies depending on the diagnostic criteria and 
imaging used. Two recent RCTs, namely the Japanese MR 
BPA trial and the French RACE trial, reported on the 
incidence of lung injury after BPA. In the MR BPA trial,
285 
lung injury (defined by CT findings, regardless of 
hypoxemia) occurred in 8 of 31 patients (26%), with 1 (3%) 
patient requiring non-invasive positive airway pressure 
ventilation. In the French RACE trial, lung injury with 
hypoxemia confirmed on chest X-ray was observed in 15% 
of patients. Furthermore, in RACE, the appearance of 
serious adverse events (including lung injury) was predicted 
by mPAP >45
 mmHg and performing BPA without
 
concomitant riociguat. As noted above, to reduce the risk 
of BPA-related lung injury, it may be beneficial to improve 
hemodynamics with medications before BPA, minimize 
procedure-related complications, and to tailor the target 
lesions to the severity of PH and lesion morphology, for 
example using a staged BPA approach
280 or guiding BPA 
with distal pressure measurements (pressure wire-guided 
BPA).
275 Rarely, a transient pulmonary edema can develop 
in lung fields outside the treated areas (delayed pulmonary 
edema). The mechanism is unclear but, like lung injury, it 
has been reported to correlate with the severity of the 
hemodynamic impairment. If severe respiratory failure 
Advance Publication
[[p37]]
RV
JCS/JPCPHS 2025 Guideline on PH and VTE
occurs due to lung injury or delayed pulmonary edema, 
supportive care with nasal high-flow oxygen, non-invasive 
positive airway pressure ventilation, mechanical ventilation, 
or extracorporeal membrane oxygenation should be used 
as appropriate to maintain adequate oxygenation.
b.
 Other Complications
Other common catheter-related complications include
 
access site complications (vessel injury, hematoma, arterio-
venous fistula), contrast reactions, and contrast-induced 
nephropathy. In patients with renal dysfunction, it is 
necessary to limit the contrast volume during BPA. 
However, one report noted that an increase in mixed venous 
oxygen saturation (SvO
2) ≥25% after BPA compared with 
before may improve renal function.286
 ▋ 2.5 BPA Outcomes
The outcomes of BPA were first reported in 2001 by a 
group in San Diego in 18 CTEPH patients who were not 
surgical candidates. In that study, mPAP improved by 
9
 mmHg, but one patient died of right heart failure
 
(mortality 5.6%).287 For approximately a decade thereafter, 
publications on BPA were mostly case reports, until 
Mizoguchi et al., from Okayama Medical Center, reported 
in 2012 that, in 68 patients, mPAP improved by 21
 mmHg
 
and mortality was 1.5%. In the subsequent approximate 10 
years, many BPA outcome studies have been published, 
mainly from Japan, and more recently reports from outside 
Japan have increased with the wider adoption of BPA.
288,289
In Japan, numerous PH specialty centers have reported 
BPA results. A multicenter registry of 308 patients who 
underwent BPA at expert centers from 2004 to 2013 showed 
that mPAP improved from a mean (±SD) of 43.2±11.0 to 
24.3±6.4
 mmHg, 
and PVR improved from 853.7±450.7 to 
359.5±222.6
 
dyn
 
·
 
s
 
·
 
cm–5 (a 58% reduction in PVR), with a 
30-day mortality of 2.6%.267 In recent data from Japanese 
high-volume BPA centers, post-BPA mPAP is around 
20
 mmHg, which is nearly normal. However, the mean
 
pre-BPA mPAP in those reports was also below 40
 mmHg,
 
indicating that, compared with earlier registries, BPA is 
now being performed in patients with less severe hemody-
namics.
290–292
Many earlier reports included patients on pulmonary 
vasodilators before and/or after BPA. The outcomes of 
BPA alone in treatment-naïve patients were demonstrated 
in the MR BPA trial from Japan, an RCT comparing BPA 
to riociguat. The primary endpoint in the MR BPA trial 
was the change in mPAP from baseline to 12 months. In 
the BPA group (n=32), mPAP decreased from 38.1±7.9 to 
21.4±4.7
 mmHg (−16.3
 mmHg), whereas in the riociguat
 
group (n=29) mPAP decreased from 37.5± 8.5 to 
31.5±7.8
 mmHg (−7.0
 mmHg). These results confirmed the
 
superior hemodynamic improvement with BPA. In that 
trial, BPA-related complications occurred in 17 of 147 
sessions (12%).
285 There have been a few reports on medium- 
and long-term outcomes after BPA. In the 308-patient 
registry, mPAP after completing BPA was 24.3±6.4
 
mmHg; 
approximately 1.2 years later it was 22.5±5.4
 
mmHg.267 In 
a study following 170 patients for a median of 2.8 years 
after BPA, hemodynamics (mPAP, PVR) remained almost 
normal at the short-term (0.5–1.5 years), mid-term (1.5–3.5 
years), and long-term (>3.5 years) follow-up.
293
The excellent results of BPA have contributed to 
improved long-term survival. Approximately 40 years ago, 
when no effective treatments beyond anticoagulation 
existed, the 5-year survival was only approximately 10% 
among patients with mPAP ≥50
 
mmHg.294 Even after PEA 
was introduced, although outcomes improved for those who 
underwent surgery, the prognosis of patients who were 
inoperable remained poor. In the 2010–15 prospective 
international CTEPH registry, the 3-year survival rate was 
89% among patients who underwent PEA, compared with 
70% among those who did not.
295
In contrast, in the Japanese BPA multicenter registry, 
the 1- and 3-year survival rates after BPA were 96.8% 
and 94.5%, respectively, which is comparable to post-PEA 
outcomes.
267 In recent years in Japan, many inoperable 
patients have undergone BPA or received medical therapy, 
and prognosis has improved along with the dissemination 
of BPA.
247,291
BPA can dramatically improve hemodynamics, and 
near-normalization of mPAP and PVR has often become 
achievable in recent years. However, the effects of BPA on 
oxygenation and lung function are limited. In the MR 
BPA trial, in treatment-naïve patients, PaO
2 improved from 
63.6±9.2 to 73.2±7.3
 mmHg and the proportion of patients
 
on long-term oxygen therapy decreased from 58% to 23%, 
but full normalization of oxygenation was not achieved.
285 
Similarly, other reports indicate that even when BPA 
almost normalizes hemodynamics, only approximately half 
of patients can discontinue home oxygen therapy.
291,293
BPA was largely developed in Japan, but with accumu-
lating evidence it has now spread worldwide and earned a 
Class I recommendation in the 2022 ESC/ERS guidelines. 
BPA outcomes differ by country, with PVR reduction rates 
ranging from 30% to 60%. Some of this variation may be 
due to patient population differences, but countries with 
high-volume PEA centers have fewer BPA cases and tend 
to report less dramatic BPA outcomes,
269,296 suggesting that 
procedural experience plays a significant role. Notably, in 
the RCTs of BPA vs. riociguat conducted in Japan and 
France, the reduction in PVR in treatment-naïve CTEPH 
was ∼59.6% and ∼59.7%, respectively, almost identical.
251,285
3. Surgical Treatment
 ▋ 3.1 Pulmonary Endarterectomy
PEA is a surgical treatment in which, under deep hypo-
thermic circulatory arrest, an endarterectomy is performed 
to remove organized thrombi along with the inner layer of 
the pulmonary artery wall in both lungs. PEA is an effective 
therapy for CTEPH with lesions that are surgically acces-
sible, and it produces immediate and marked improvements 
in pulmonary hemodynamics, symptoms, and exercise 
capacity after surgery. In an international registry, PVR 
decreased from 736 to 248
 
dyn
 
·
 
s
 
·
 
cm–5 (median), the 
proportion of patients with WHO Class I/II improved from 
25% preoperatively to 90% postoperatively, and the 
6-minute walk distance (6MWD) increased from 362 to 
459
 
m (median).297
In Japan, the annual number of PEA surgeries from 
2012 to 2021was 50–70 per year, remaining unchanged 
over that period
298,299 (Figure 1 1). A 2011 Japanese registry 
study found that only 13.9% of CTEPH patients underwent 
PEA, compared with 56.8% in the 2007–09 international 
registry.
297,300 In a 2015–16 international registry, among all 
CTEPH patients, only 25% in Japan were judged operable, 
compared with 72% in Europe and 69% in the US.
301
Advance Publication
[[p38]]
38
TAMURA Y et al.
 ▋ 3.2 Indications
Indications for surgery are determined by factors including 
the team’s experience, the surgical accessibility of the 
lesions, the relationship between PH severity and lesion 
location/extent, and the presence of comorbidities.
7,250 
Pulmonary angiography and CT can underestimate the 
extent of disease, so evaluation of surgical accessibility by 
an experienced PEA surgeon is crucial.
302 Although a 
high preoperative PVR is associated with increased risk of 
perioperative mortality, such patients should not be excluded 
from surgery, because the improvements in pulmonary 
circulation with PEA can be substantial.
303 Lesions at the 
segmental branch level overlap with areas treatable by BPA, 
but segmental lesions on the proximal side are considered 
surgically accessible. Some PEA experts consider distal 
segmental lesions to be accessible.
302,304 Furthermore, even 
in patients without PH (CTEPD without PH), PEA can be 
contemplated if the patient is symptomatic and has 
surgically accessible thrombotic lesions.
255
 ▋ 3.3 Pulmonary Artery Lesion Location (Morphology)
Two classifications are commonly used: the Jamieson 
classification and the University of California, San Diego 
(UCSD) classification. The Jamieson classification 
categorizes the morphology of surgical specimens: Types I 
and II are central-type disease, and Types III and IV are 
peripheral-type disease.
305 The UCSD classification is 
based on the location of the predominant lesions: Levels I 
and II are central, and Levels III and IV are peripheral.
306
The PEA procedure is performed under general anesthesia 
via a median sternotomy, using cardiopulmonary bypass 
and deep hypothermic circulatory arrest (core temperature 
lowered to <20°C) as needed. The surgeon establishes a 
dissection plane in the media just beneath the intima in the 
main pulmonary arteries, and then removes the thrombotic 
intima specimen extending to the subsegmental branch 
level. The deep hypothermic circulatory arrest technique 
is used to provide a bloodless surgical field during the 
endarterectomy.
250,305
 ▋ 3.4 PEA Outcomes
  ▋ 3.4.1 Early Outcomes
PEA outcomes depend on the experience of the surgeon
 
and the multidisciplinary team. At centers performing 
≥100 PEA procedures annually, the 30-day in-hospital 
mortality rate is approximately 2–3%.
250,307–309
An international registry study found that centers 
performing ≥50 PEA procedures per year had lower hospital 
and 1-year mortality rates than centers performing <10 PEA 
procedures per year (hospital mortality 4.7% vs. 7.4%, 
respectively; 1-year mortality 7% vs. 11.1%, respectively).
297 
In Japan, the 30-day (in-hospital) mortality for PEA was 
3.65% (4.75% including in-hospital deaths) in the 10-year 
period from 2012 to 2021, and this improved to 2.6% (4.5% 
including in-hospital deaths) in the more recent 5-year 
period from 2017 to 2021
298 (Figure  11).
Reported risk factors for in-hospital mortality include 
high preoperative PVR, WHO Class IV, right heart failure, 
and high postoperative PVR.
250,297,310,311 There is no evidence 
that preoperative pulmonary vasodilator therapy improves 
surgical outcomes; some reports have even suggested that 
prolonging time to surgery (to treat medically) was 
associated with higher early and long-term mortality.
249,295 
In the case of mixed-type disease (one lung with central 
disease and the contralateral lung with distal disease), a 
strategy has been reported in which BPA is performed on 
the peripheral lesions before surgery to reduce surgical 
risk, followed by PEA for the central lesions.
312
Complications specific to PEA include pulmonary 
(airway) hemorrhage, reperfusion pulmonary edema, and 
residual PH, and are the main causes of in-hospital 
death.
297 In addition, deep hypothermic circulatory arrest 
carries a risk of neurological complications.313–315
  ▋ 3.4.2 Late Outcomes
In an international registry, 1- and 3-year survival rates
 
after PEA were 93% and 89%, respectively, significantly 
better than the 88% and 70%, respectively, observed in the 
non-operated patient group.
295 Single-center studies have 
reported 5- and 10-year survival rates of approximately

for late mortality include residual PH after PEA and 
preoperative WHO Class IV.
249,295 Cannon et al. reported 
that mPAP ≥ 38
 mmHg and PVR 
≥425
 
dyn
 
·
 
s
 
·
 
cm–5 after 
PEA were risk factors for long-term all-cause mortality.249
Ishida et al. studied patients who survived beyond 30 days 
after PEA in the era of multimodal treatment, comparing 
patients with and without residual PH immediately after 
PEA. Those patients with residual PH were treated with 
pulmonary vasodilators and/or BPA. The authors found 
that residual PH immediately after PEA did not affect 
CTEPH-related mortality.
316 This supports a multimodal 
approach to managing residual PH after PEA.

4. Medical Treatment
 ▋ 4.1 Pulmonary Vasodilator Therapy in CTEPH
  ▋ 4.1.1 Background and Rationale for Use of Oral Therapies
CTEPH is caused by organized thrombi that physically
 
narrow and occlude pulmonary arteries, but the pathology 
of CTEPH involves a pulmonary arteriopathy (microvas-
culopathy) similar to that in pulmonary arterial hypertension 
(PAH).
317 Therefore, it has been hypothesized that pulmo-
nary vasodilators used for PAH could also be effective in 
CTEPH. The microvasculopathy in CTEPH cannot be 
addressed by PEA or BPA. Accordingly, researchers have 
investigated medical therapy in patients who are inoperable 
or who have residual PH after PEA. These efforts demon-
strated the efficacy and safety of PAH therapies in CTEPH, 
and, in Japan, the soluble guanylate cyclase stimulator 
riociguat
318 and the prostacyclin receptor agonist selexipag318 
have been approved for CTEPH. These medications are 
indicated for patients who are inoperable or who have 
residual or recurrent PH after PEA. There is currently no 
evidence on initial combination oral therapy (e.g., riociguat 
plus selexipag) in CTEPH, and the results of future studies 
are awaited. Moreover, if interventions like BPA or PEA 
normalize the pulmonary hemodynamics, the clinical value 
of continuing PAH medications is unclear due to a lack of 
evidence, and further research is needed.
  ▋ 4.1.2 Riociguat
The CHEST-1 trial was an RCT of riociguat that showed
 
a significant improvement in the 6MWD in both inoperable 
CTEPH (70% of patients) and in patients with persistent/
recurrent CTEPH after PEA (30% of patients).
318 This 
improvement was correlated with a significant reduction in 
PVR and improvements in right ventricular function 
parameters.
316 The improvement in 6MWD was sustained 
at 2 years in 98% of patients who enrolled in the long-term 
extension (CHEST-2).
319 As a result, riociguat became the 
first pulmonary vasodilator approved for CTEPH in 
patients who are not candidates for PEA or who have 
residual PH after PEA. In addition, in the MR BPA trial 
(an RCT in Japan comparing BPA to riociguat),
285 although 
BPA yielded a greater decrease in mPAP, riociguat produced 
a significant increase in cardiac output. This effect was also 
observed in the RACE trial (an RCT in France evaluating 
BPA vs. riociguat in terms of PVR reduction). Furthermore, 
a subanalysis of RACE showed that initiating riociguat 
before BPA reduced the incidence of serious BPA-related 
adverse events, which provides a rationale for recommending 
riociguat pretreatment.
7
  ▋ 4.1.3 Selexipag
In a phase I
I trial in Japanese patients, selexipag did not 
show a significant improvement in the primary endpoint 
(PVR), but there was a trend towards improved pulmonary 
hemodynamics.
320 Therefore, a phase III trial in Japanese 
CTEPH patients was conducted, 252 which demonstrated 
that the primary endpoint (change in PVR from baseline) 
improved significantly with selexipag vs. placebo, leading 
to the listing of selexipag in Japan for CTEPH in 2021.
 ▋ 4.2 Anticoagulation Therapy in CTEPH
In the treatment of CTEPH, anticoagulation is the most 
fundamental pharmacotherapy, and it must be continued 
lifelong. The 2022 ESC/ERS guidelines recommend warfarin 
(Class I, Level C) as the anticoagulant for CTEPH,
7 but no 
prospective studies have demonstrated whether anticoagu-
lation improves prognosis or prevents CTEPH progression.
DOACs are used as first-line therapy for acute PE and 
for the secondary prevention of venous thromboembolism, 
and, in real-world practice, many patients who develop 
CTEPH after PE are maintained on DOACs. However, 
experience with the use of DOACs specifically in CTEPH 
is limited, and their efficacy and safety have not been 
confirmed.
In a retrospective multicenter study from the UK of 
CTEPH patients after PEA, the recurrence rate of venous 
thromboembolism was higher among those on DOACs 
than among those on warfarin (4.6% vs. 0.8% per patient-
year, respectively), but the incidence of major bleeding 
(0.7% per patient-year in both groups) and 5-year survival 
(90% vs. 92%, respectively) were similar between the 2 
groups. Because VTE recurrences were particularly common 
within 6 months after surgery in that study, caution may 
be warranted when using DOACs in the early postoperative 
period.
245 Results from a registry study in Turkey suggest 
that DOACs may carry a lower risk of major bleeding.321
In the international post-marketing prospective EXPERT 
registry of CTEPH patients on riociguat, the incidence of 
major thrombotic events was 1.6% (17 events/1,068 patient-
years) in the vitamin K antagonist (including warfarin) 
group vs. 2.9% (8 events/276 patient-years) in the DOACs 
group, which was not a statistically significant difference 
but suggested a possible increase in thrombotic events with 
DOACs.
246 Conversely, data from the Japanese CTEPH 
AC registry showed that DOACs did not increase the 
risk of worsening CTEPH compared with warfarin and 
tended to reduce the incidence of clinically relevant bleeding 
events.
247
Japanese investigators conducted the world’s first head-
to-head RCT directly comparing warfarin and a DOAC in 
CTEPH patients, known as the KABUKI trial.
244 In that 
trial, edoxaban was shown to be non-inferior to warfarin 
for the primary endpoint (the ratio of PVR change after 48 
weeks of treatment). The incidence of clinically important 
???SWL????P??RV?
?GQMVDH??????Q??RV??
(5.4%) in the warfarin group, with no symptomatic venous 
thromboembolism or deaths recorded in either group. 
Based on this evidence, edoxaban is considered an effective 
and safe anticoagulant therapy comparable to warfarin in 
CTEPH patients after revascularization therapy (PEA or 
BPA). In the future, it is expected that the use of DOACs 
in CTEPH will increase worldwide. It will be important to 
promptly establish large registries of CTEPH patients on 
warfarin and DOACs, and to conduct comparative registry 
studies and prospective RCTs (with warfarin as the control) 
to further evaluate the efficacy and safety of DOACs in 
CTEPH.
5. Peripheral Pulmonary Stenosis and Vasculitis 
(Recommendation Table
 
21)
 ▋ 5.1 Peripheral Pulmonary Stenosis
Peripheral pulmonary stenosis (PPS) is characterized by 
multiple stenoses in the intrapulmonary arteries. Most 
pediatric cases of PPS are associated with congenital 
syndromes such as congenital rubella syndrome, Williams 
Advance Publication
[[p40]]
40
TAMURA Y et al.
syndrome, or Noonan syndrome, or with structural cardiac 
anomalies such as ventricular septal defect. PPS is also 
known to occur in association with moyamoya disease, 
and a relationship with RNF213, the susceptibility gene for 
moyamoya disease, has been reported.
322,323
Sporadic adult-onset PPS without other congenital 
disorders has been described. 324–327 In a Japanese study, 
84% of patients had CTEPH-like wedge-shaped perfusion 
defects on V/Q scans. Pulmonary angiography showed 
stenosis in segmental arteries (93%), subsegmental arteries 
(82%), and tortuosity (43%)
324 (Figure  12A). Of 20 Japanese 
patients diagnosed with adult-onset PPS, 80% carried 
RNF213 mutations, most being homozygous mutations.
327
No standardized treatment exists for PPS. Japanese 
reports show that although pulmonary vasodilators alone 
improve hemodynamics, greater benefit is achieved with 
catheter intervention. Several studies have reported improved 
hemodynamics and symptoms after catheter therapy in 
adult-onset PPS. However, compared with CTEPH, the 
response to balloon angioplasty is limited due to incomplete 
lesion dilation and restenosis.
327
 ▋ 5.2  Pulmonary Hypertension Associated With 
Vasculitis
Takayasu arteritis is a granulomatous vasculitis of unknown 
cause that primarily affects the aorta and its major branches 
and is more prevalent in Asian women.
328 Isolated pulmo-
nary artery involvement is rare, but approximately 8.3% of 
patients with Takayasu arteritis have pulmonary artery 
lesions, including dilation of the main pulmonary artery, 
stenosis or occlusion of the right or left pulmonary arteries, 
and thrombotic lesions
329,330 (Figure  12B). PH in Takayasu 
arteritis is a prognostic factor and occurs in 61.7% of 
patients with pulmonary artery lesions.
329,330
Evaluation of disease activity is critical and requires 
collaboration with rheumatologists. Diagnostic tools 
include digital subtraction angiography, magnetic resonance 
angiography, CTPA, positron emission tomography CT, 
and V/Q scans. CTPA and pulmonary angiography typically 
show no organized thrombi, unlike CTEPH. Differential 
diagnosis via V/Q scans alone is challenging.
Reported 1-, 3-, and 5-year survival rates for patients 
with Takayasu arteritis and PH are 94.0%, 83.2%, and 
77.2%, respectively.
331 Treatment options for Takayasu 
arteritis-related PH include percutaneous pulmonary angio-
plasty and pulmonary vasodilators.
332 Immunosuppressive 
therapy should be considered in patients with active 
vasculitis.
333

by idiopathic/heritable PAH (I/HPAH) and PAH associated 
with congenital heart disease. Developmental lung disorders 
such as bronchopulmonary dysplasia and congenital

diaphragmatic hernia account for 34–49% of cases. 335–337 
Genetic developmental lung disorders, including alveolar 
capillary dysplasia and TBX4 variant-related lung disease, 
are also categorized within this group.
338 Another feature 
of pediatric PH is a high proportion of chromosomal 
abnormalities, such as trisomy 21.
338 The annual incidence 
of pediatric I/HPAH is reported to be 0.48–0.7 per 
1,000,000 population, and the prevalence is 2.1–2.2 per 
1,000,000 population.
334,339,340 According to the Japanese 
Society of Pediatric Cardiology and Cardiac Surgery survey 
on heart disease with onset in childhood,
341 the number of 
cases of pediatric I/HPAH in Japan in recent years has 
been 24–43 per year, suggesting an incidence of approxi-
mately 1–2 per 1,000,000 population. The femaletomale 
ratio is relatively low in children (0.9–1.6) compared with 
that in adults (1.7–4.1).
70,334,342–348
 ▋ 1.2 HPAH in Children
As in adults, pathogenic variants are identified in 20–30% 
of sporadic cases of pediatric PAH and in 70–80% of 
familial cases.
42,349 Although BMPR2 variants are the most 
frequent, a characteristic of pediatric PAH is the higher 
frequency of variants in ACVRL1
334 and TBX4.350–352 TBX4 
is a gene involved in lung and skeletal development,353 and 
its carrier frequency is the second most common after that 
of BMPR2.
349 Although cases cluster in infancy and early 
childhood, TBX4 variants have also been identified in adult 
PAH and in Group 3 PH (developmental lung disorders).354 
Variants in BMPR2 and ACVRL1 have been reported as 
prognostic factors even in children.
355 Genetic counseling 
and genetic testing are recommended for pediatric PH 
patients and their families.
356
 ▋ 1.3 Diagnosis and Risk Assessment
  ▋ 1.3.1 Diagnosis
C
ommon symptoms of pediatric I/HPAH include exertional 
dyspnea (44–66%) and easy fatigability (25–55%), whereas 
syncope is observed in 18–36% of patients.
336,347,348 Because 
the symptoms are nonspecific,70,342,343,357 there is a tendency 
towards delayed diagnosis,336,347 and prior to the diagnosis 
of PAH, patients are sometimes diagnosed with conditions 
such as asthma or epilepsy.
358 In Japan, approximately 
onethird of I/HPAH cases are detected through school 
electrocardiographic screening; many of these patients 
are relatively asymptomatic and are in World Health 
Organization (WHO) Class I or II, but it should be noted 
that they are not necessarily mild cases.
348 Compared with 
adults, pediatric I/HPAH is characterized by higher PAP 
but a preserved cardiac index, lower right atrial pressure, 
and relatively preserved exercise tolerance; in addition, 
marked dyspnea or syncope tends to occur with excessive 
exertion.
359,360
  ▋ 1.3.2 Severity Assessment
Key variables for evaluating severity and prognosis in 
pediatric I/HPAH are presented in 
Table
 
13.361–363 Based 
on these variables, patients are categorized into low and 
highrisk groups, and the treatment strategy is determined 
(Figure
 
13).362 It is recommended that treatment be selected 
with the goal of achieving and maintaining a lowrisk profile 
as evidence of adequate therapeutic effect. Serum B-type 
natriuretic peptide and N-terminal pro B-type natriuretic 
peptide levels,
364–366 as well as serum uric acid levels,367 are 
used for to assess severity and evaluate treatment response.

2. PH in the Neonatal Period
Physiological PH that exists immediately after birth 
decreases stepwise over time, with pulmonary arterial 
pressure becoming normal by around 3 months of age. 
Pathological PH occurring in the neonatal period includes 
persistent PH of the newborn (PPHN), PH associated with 
bronchopulmonary dysplasia/newborn chronic lung disease, 
and PH associated with congenital diaphragmatic hernia.
 ▋ 2.1 Symptoms and Diagnosis of PPHN
In PPHN, marked hypoxemia is present, with a difference 
in oxygen saturation between the upper and lower 
extremities (oxygen saturation in the lower extremities being 
≥5–10% lower than in the upper extremities). Echocar-
diography is essential for the assessment of cardiovascular 
structure, cardiac function, and hemodynamics; in severe 
PPHN, righttoleft (or bidirectional) shunting is observed 
at both the ductus arteriosus and the foramen ovale. In the 
differential diagnosis, it is important to consider coexisting 
cyanotic congenital heart disease and to be alert for alveolar 
capillary dysplasia.
 ▋ 2.2 PH-Specific Therapy for PPHN
Inhaled nitric oxide (iNO) reduces the incidence of death 
or initiation of extracorporeal membrane oxygenation in

VIII. Treatment of Pulmonary Embolism
PM?s?r?`???
the Severity of Acute Pulmonary Embolism
In recent years, treatment strategies based on detailed 
severity classifications, combining hemodynamic parameters, 
imaging findings, biomarkers, and clinical scoring systems 
such as the Pulmonary Embolism Severity Index (PESI) and 
Simplified PESI (sPESI), as recommended by the European 
Society of Cardiology (ESC) guidelines, have become 
widely accepted.
2 A flowchart outlining the treatment 
strategies for acute pulmonary embolism (PE) according 
to severity is shown in Figure
 
14.2
In cases of cardiac arrest, immediate consideration should 
be given to the introduction of veno-arterial extracorporeal 
membrane oxygenation (VA-ECMO). For high-risk 
patients in shock, in addition to anticoagulation therapy, 
reperfusion therapies such as thrombolytic therapy, surgical 
embolectomy, or catheter-directed treatments should be 
considered. For intermediate-risk patients who present 
with both right ventricular (RV) dysfunction and elevated 
cardiac biomarkers (intermediate–high risk), anticoagu-
lation should be initiated along with close hemodynamic 
monitoring. If hemodynamic deterioration occurs, reper-
fusion therapy should be considered. For patients at 
intermediate–low risk, hospitalization and initiation of 
anticoagulation therapy should be considered. Conversely, 
for low-risk patients identified using clinical risk scores 
such as sPESI and without evidence of RV dysfunction, 
anticoagulation therapy can be initiated with consideration 
of early discharge or outpatient treatment.
2. Outpatient Treatment for Acute PE
Traditionally, acute PE has been managed with careful 
inpatient treatment. However, the widespread availability 
of direct oral anticoagulants (DOACs) such as rivaroxaban 
and apixaban has enabled treatment with oral medication 
from the acute phase, allowing not only for early discharge 
but also for outpatient treatment. Therefore, identifying 
appropriate candidates for outpatient care using acute-
phase risk scores is important.
The Hestia score, developed with a focus on selecting 
patients for outpatient treatment, includes both patient 
clinical background and social factors conducive to 
outpatient care. Its utility has been demonstrated in several 
studies.
404,405
Although the PESI/sPESI scores were originally designed 
to stratify 30-day mortality risk, they are now also used to 
assess eligibility for outpatient treatment. In a randomized 
controlled trial (RCT) of 344 low-risk patients identified 
by the PESI score, those assigned to inpatient vs. outpatient 
treatment had the same 90-day mortality rate of 0.6%, 
suggesting outpatient management is feasible for properly 
selected patients.
406
In a Japanese cohort, the 30-day mortality rate in patients 
with an sPESI score of 0 was 0.5%, significantly lower than 
?????≥PM
407 These findings support extrapo-
lating Western data to Japanese patients for outpatient 
treatment.
Recent studies have also examined outpatient treatment 
using DOACs. Among 525 low-risk PE patients who were 
hemodynamically stable, had no RV dysfunction, and no 
serious comorbidities, outpatient treatment with rivar-
oxaban resulted in no PE-related deaths and only 3 (0.6%) 
cases of symptomatic or fatal recurrent PE at 3 months.
408
Although criteria for patient selection remain under 
discussion, outpatient treatment using DOACs may be a 
reasonable option for patients with a low-risk profile, 
favorable social circumstances, and no significant comor-
bidities. The 2019 ESC guidelines state that outpatient 
treatment can be considered for patients judged to be at low 
risk based on clinical scores and without RV dysfunction, 
provided they have appropriate social support.
2
In such cases, appropriate evaluation of residual deep 
vein thrombosis (DVT) and careful attention to recurrence 
risk are necessary. Recently, incidental or mild PE has 
been increasingly identified in cancer patients. Although 
cancer contributes 1 point to the sPESI score, making 
cancer patients them formally ineligible for a “low-risk” 
status, some recent studies suggest that outpatient care 
may still be feasible for cancer patients without additional 
sPESI factors.
409
3. Respiratory and Circulatory Management of 
High-Risk PE (Recommendation Table
 
25)
Acute PE is fundamentally a form of acute respiratory and 
circulatory failure. In massive PE, the mortality rate within 
Advance Publication
[[p45]]
45
JCS/JPCPHS 2025 Guideline on PH and VTE
the first hour is extremely high.410 In severe cases, respiratory 
and circulatory management, diagnosis, and treatment 
must proceed simultaneously.
 ▋ 3.1 Circulatory Management
Circulatory failure in acute PE is primarily due to 
mechanical obstruction of the pulmonary vascular bed 
and pulmonary vasoconstriction caused by hypoxia and 
chemical mediators, leading to increased pulmonary 
vascular resistance (PVR), pulmonary hypertension, and 
increased RV afterload. Clinically, this manifests as elevated 
pulmonary arterial pressure, elevated RV and right atrial 
pressures, reduced RV output (RV failure), reduced left 
ventricular (LV) output, systemic hypotension, and shock.
 ▋ 3.2 Volume Loading
There is no strong evidence supporting volume loading in 
PE. Some experimental models show conflicting results.
411,412 
Goldhaber reported that volume loading of the RV could 
impair LV filling via ventricular interdependence and 
further reduce cardiac output.
413 The 2019 ESC guidelines 
suggest that in patients with low central venous pressure, a 
cautious fluid challenge (≤ 500
 mL) may improve cardiac
 
output.414

[[p46]]

 ▋ 3.3 Pharmacologic Therapy
Vasopressors with sympathomimetic effects (norepinephrine, 
dopamine, dobutamine) and phosphodiesterase 3 inhibitors 
with positive inotropic and vasodilatory properties can be 
used. Norepinephrine improves systemic hemodynamics 
without affecting PVR by enhancing ventricular contractility 
and coronary perfusion.
412 In an RCT comparing 
norepinephrine to dopamine in shock, norepinephrine 
was associated with fewer arrhythmias and lower 28-day 
mortality in PE patients.
415
In small studies, dobutamine reportedly improved cardiac 
index by 35% and reduced PVR.416 Although some patients 
show a drop in PaO2 due to ventilation–perfusion mismatch, 
overall oxygen delivery improves with increased cardiac 
output.
417 Milrinone, a phosphodiesterase 3 inhibitor, has 
shown favorable pulmonary vasodilation without worsening 
oxygenation in canine models of PE and may be more 
selective than dopamine or dobutamine.
418 Current practice 
generally favors dobutamine for RV failure without hypo-
tension and norepinephrine when hypotension is present.
 ▋ 3.4 Inhaled Nitric Oxide
Inhaled nitric oxide (NO) selectively dilates pulmonary 
arteries without significantly affecting systemic vascular 
resistance, theoretically improving ventilation–perfusion 
mismatch in acute PE. Some case reports show improved 
pulmonary artery pressure and PVR with NO inhalation,
419,420 
but evidence regarding clinical efficacy and safety remains 
insufficient.
421 In Japan, inhaled NO is covered by medical 
insurance only during perioperative cardiac care.
 ▋ 3.5 Mechanical Circulatory Support
Mechanical support should be considered in patients 
presenting with cardiac arrest, those who fail to stabilize 
with oxygen therapy and medication, and those with 
progressive hypoxemia or hypotension. VA-ECMO can 
rapidly stabilize respiratory and circulatory function and 
serve as a bridge to definitive therapy.
2 After extracorporeal 
membrane oxygenation (ECMO) initiation, transesophageal 
echocardiography or contrast computed tomography 
should be performed to confirm thrombi near the main 
pulmonary arteries, and surgical embolectomy should be 
considered.
According to data from the Japanese Society for 
Cardiovascular Surgery (2008–14), among 355 patients 
undergoing surgical embolectomy, 94 received ECMO, 
and the 30-day mortality rate in this group was 24.5%.
422 
Other studies show high survival rates in PE patients with 
ECMO support during cardiogenic shock or arrest.
423 
However, a meta-analysis up to 2020 did not show definitive 
survival benefits.
424 The optimal method of reperfusion in 
cases where VA-ECMO is initiated remains unclear.425–427 
Key predictors of survival with ECMO include age under 
60 years and receiving surgical embolectomy.
428 Conversely, 
cardiac arrest before ECMO initiation was associated with 
poorer outcomes. Care must be taken to prevent thrombus 
obstruction of ECMO drainage cannulas if thrombi remain 
in the right atrium or inferior vena cava (IVC).
429 The 2020 
Japan Resuscitation Council guidelines suggest considering 
ECMO-assisted cardiopulmonary resuscitation when 
conventional resuscitation fails and appropriate indications 
are present.
430
4. Thrombolytic Therapy for PE  
(Recommendation Table
 
26)
Thrombolytic therapy can dissolve thrombi and promptly 
improve the pulmonary circulation more effectively than 
anticoagulation alone with unfractionated heparin.
431 In 
Japan, monteplase (a recombinant tissue plasminogen 
activator) is the only thrombolytic agent covered by medical 
insurance for the treatment of acute PE. Thrombolytic 
therapy shows its greatest efficacy when administered 
within 48
 h of symptom onset, although it may still be
 
beneficial in cases with symptoms lasting 6–14 days. 432 
Although thrombolytic therapy clearly provides rapid 
thrombus resolution and hemodynamic improvement, 
RCTs have not demonstrated a significant survival benefit.
A meta-analysis of 16 RCTs involving PE patients 
(10% low risk, 71% intermediate risk, 1% high risk, 18% 
unclassified) reported that thrombolytic therapy was 
associated with a reduction in all-cause mortality (odds ratio 
[OR] 0.53; 95% confidence interval [CI] 0.32–0.88; P=0.01) 
but also with a significantly increased risk of major bleeding 
(OR 2.73; 95% CI 1.91–3.91; P<0.01).
433 However, most of 
these studies did not target high-risk patients. Thus, it 
remains unclear whether anticoagulation alone is sufficient 
in severe cases.

COR, Class of Recommendation; LOE, Level of Evidence; PaO
2, arterial partial oxygen pressure; SpO2, percutaneous 
oxygen saturation; V-A ECMO, veno-arterial extracorporeal membrane oxygenation.
Advance Publication
[[p47]]
47
JCS/JPCPHS 2025 Guideline on PH and VTE
Jerjes-Sanchez et al. demonstrated in the only RCT of 
acute PE with shock that all 4 patients who received throm-
bolytic therapy survived, whereas all 4 in the heparin-only 
group died within 1–3
 h.434
Right ventricular dysfunction at onset is considered an 
important prognostic factor in acute PE. The PEITHO 
trial, a multicenter double-blind RCT conducted in 2014, 
evaluated thrombolytic therapy (tenecteplase
 +
 heparin) vs.
 
anticoagulation alone (placebo+heparin) in 1,005 normo-
tensive patients with RV dysfunction and myocardial injury 
(elevated troponin).
435 The combined endpoint of death or 
hemodynamic deterioration within 7 days occurred less 
frequently in the thrombolysis than anticoagulation alone 
group (2.6% vs. 5.6%; P=0.015), but there was no difference 
in all-cause mortality (1.2% vs. 1.8%, respectively; P=0.42). 
Major extracranial bleeding (6.3% vs. 1.2%) and stroke, 
including intracranial hemorrhage (2.4% vs. 0.2%), were 
significantly more common in the thrombolysis group, 
offsetting the efficacy benefit.
Based on these results, routine thrombolytic therapy is 
not recommended for hemodynamically stable PE patients 
with RV dysfunction. However, it may be considered in 
younger patients at low risk of bleeding or in cases of 
hemodynamic worsening despite anticoagulation.
Bleeding is the major complication of thrombolytic 
therapy. Post-marketing surveillance of monteplase in 
Japan of 1,241 patients reported major bleeding in 8.1% 
and intracranial hemorrhage in 1.7%.
436 There are absolute 
and relative contraindications to thrombolytic therapy 
due to bleeding risks (Table
 
14).2 Many relative contrain-
dications overlap with PE risk factors, requiring careful 
consideration. When thrombolytic therapy is contrain-
dicated, surgical or catheter-directed embolectomy or 
placement of an IVC filter should be considered as alter-
native strategies.
There is no clear consensus regarding the need for 
concurrent heparin during thrombolytic therapy, because 
its effect on efficacy or bleeding risk remains inconclusive.
Recent studies have explored half-dose thrombolysis. In 
small RCTs comparing half-dose thrombolysis to full-dose 
therapy or anticoagulation alone, half-dose thrombolysis 
showed comparable efficacy and safety.
437,438 However, a 
meta-analysis of 4 observational studies and 9 RCTs 
indicated that full-dose thrombolysis carried a higher risk 
of bleeding than low-dose thrombolysis (OR 1.48; 95% CI 
1.00–2.19), and that systemic low-dose thrombolysis 
resulted in a 5-fold increase in bleeding compared with 
anticoagulation alone (relative risk [RR] 5.08; 95% CI 
1.39–18.6).
439
Whether thrombolytic therapy influences long-term 
outcomes such as functional capacity, symptom persistence, 
or the development of chronic thromboembolic pulmonary 
hypertension in high- or intermediate-risk PE remains 
uncertain. One small RCT suggested that thrombolysis 
may improve functional capacity at 3 months.
440 In the 
PEITHO trial, 33% of patients had mild symptoms (mainly 
dyspnea) at long-term follow-up (∼ 41.6 months),
441 but 
persistent or newly developed pulmonary hypertension on 
echocardiography was rare in both treatment arms 
(tenecteplase: 85%; placebo: 96%).
5. Anticoagulation Therapy for PE 
(Recommendation Table
 
27)
 ▋ 5.1 Historical Background in Japan
In Japan, the only standard anticoagulation therapy for 
venous thromboembolism (VTE) had been unfractionated 
heparin as an injectable agent and warfarin as an oral 
anticoagulant. In contrast, low-molecular-weight heparin 
(LMWH), which does not require dose adjustment, has 
long been available as the first-line injectable anticoagulant 
in Western countries. However, in Japan, LMWH is still 
not covered by health insurance for the treatment or 
secondary prevention of VTE, except in some perioperative 
prophylactic settings in orthopedic and abdominal surgery. 
Fondaparinux, an indirect synthetic Factor Xa inhibitor 
administered subcutaneously, became available in Japan in 
2011. Following this, direct oral Factor Xa inhibitors, 
namely edoxaban, rivaroxaban, and apixaban, became 
available from 2014 onwards.

 ▋ 5.2  Secondary Prevention and Anticoagulation in 
the Chronic Phase
Once the acute phase resolves, the next step is to consider 
anticoagulation therapy for secondary prevention of VTE 
recurrence. Because anticoagulants carry a bleeding risk, 
the therapy should be reserved for patients whose risk of 
recurrence outweighs the bleeding risk. These risks may 
change over time, so it is essential to re-evaluate them 
regularly and weigh the risks and benefits of continuing or 
discontinuing anticoagulation therapy. Furthermore, in 
assessing these risks and benefits, the specific clinical 
weight and consequences of recurrence or bleeding events 
should also be considered.
 ▋ 5.3  Evidence on the Optimal Duration of 
Anticoagulation
Historically, numerous RCTs have been conducted to 
determine the optimal duration of warfarin therapy.
442–446 
These studies yielded several insights: (i) all patients should 
receive at least 3 months of anticoagulation; (ii) whether 
the treatment duration is 3–6 months or longer (12–24 
months), the risk of recurrence after discontinuation is 
similar; and (iii) extending anticoagulation beyond 3 
months significantly reduces the risk of recurrence, but this 
benefit may be offset by an increased bleeding risk.
More recently, RCTs on DOACs, believed to have a 
lower bleeding risk than warfarin, have also been reported, 
including the EINSTEIN Extension trial,
447 EINSTEIN 
Choice trial,448 and the AMPLIFY Extension trial. 449 In 
the EINSTEIN Extension trial, 447 of the 1,196 patients 
with first-time DVT who had completed 6–12 months of 
anticoagulation, those randomized to an additional 6–12 
months of rivaroxaban (20
 mg/day) had a significantly 
lower recurrence rate than those on placebo (1.3% vs.
 
7.1%; hazard ration [HR] 0.18). However, major or 
clinically relevant bleeding occurred more frequently in the 
rivaroxaban than placebo group (6.0% vs. 1.2%). In the 
EINSTEIN Choice trial,
448 a double-blind RCT with 3,365 
symptomatic VTE patients who had completed 6–12 
months of anticoagulation, those given rivaroxaban 10 or 
20
 mg daily for an additional 12 months had significantly
 
lower recurrence rates than those given aspirin 100
 mg
 
daily (1.2% and 1.5% vs. 4.4%, respectively; HR 0.26 
[10
 mg 
rivaroxaban] and 0.34 [20
 mg 
rivaroxaban]), with 
comparable bleeding risks. In the AMPLIFY Extension 
trial,
449 among 2,486 patients with a first episode of VTE, 
12 additional months of apixaban 2.5 or 5
 mg twice daily
 
reduced all-cause mortality and symptomatic VTE recur-
rence vs. placebo (3.8% and 4.2% vs. 11.6%, respectively; 
HR 0.33 [2.5
 mg apixaban] and 0.36 [5
 mg apixaban]). 
There were no significant differences in major or clinically
 
relevant bleeding.
 ▋ 5.4  Perspectives From International Guidelines on 
the Duration of Anticoagulation
Clinical trials to date have shown that extending antico-
agulation therapy beyond 3 months significantly reduces 
the risk of VTE recurrence, although it slightly increases 
bleeding risk. However, because DOACs are associated 
with a potentially lower bleeding risk, recent international 
guidelines recommend prolonged anticoagulation for 
patients not considered to have a low recurrence risk.
2,3
Low-dose rivaroxaban (10 m g) and apixaban (2.5 m g twice 
daily) have demonstrated efficacy in preventing recurrent 
VTE while maintaining a favorable bleeding profile. As a 
result, extended treatment with low-dose anticoagulants is 
recommended for non-cancer patients with VTE.
2,3
However, such low-dose regimens are not yet covered by 
insurance in Japan, and clinical evidence specific to the

Japanese population remains limited. 450,451 Given that 
Japanese and other Asian populations may have a higher 
bleeding risk than Western populations, the appropriateness 
of low-dose extended therapy may, in fact, be greater in 
Japan, warranting further investigation.
 ▋ 5.5 Aspirin as an Alternative Therapy
Aspirin has antiplatelet effects and is inferior to antico-
agulants in preventing recurrent VTE, but it carries a 
lower bleeding risk and has shown utility for primary VTE 
prevention in orthopedic postoperative settings. In 2 RCTs 
in which aspirin was administered after the completion of 
standard anticoagulation therapy in patients with unpro-
voked VTE, the recurrence risk was reduced by approxi-
mately 30–35%.
452,453
Although aspirin was less effective than anticoagulants 
(less than half as effective), the increased bleeding risk was 
minimal. Thus, although anticoagulation remains the 
first-line therapy for secondary prevention, aspirin may be 
considered as an alternative in patients who refuse or 
cannot tolerate anticoagulants.
2
6. Risk of VTE Recurrence and  
Duration of Therapy
 ▋ 6.1 Basic Risk Estimation of VTE Recurrence
Estimating the risk of recurrence after discontinuing 
anticoagulation is essential for determining the appropriate 
treatment duration. Studies have shown that clinical features 
at the time of VTE onset significantly influence the risk of 
recurrence.
454,455 An initial step involves categorizing the 
event based on provoking factors: transient major provoking 
factors (e.g., surgery) are associated with very low recurrence 
risk once removed; persistent provoking factors (e.g., 
active cancer) carry a high recurrence risk. Unprovoked 
VTE falls between these extremes and is considered to have 
a moderate risk. Many guidelines now classify VTE 
patients into 5 groups based on recurrence risk. Although 
classification criteria and estimated risk vary slightly across 
sources, a representative classification is presented in 
Table
 
15.
 ▋ 6.2  Additional Risk Factors to Consider for 
Recurrence
In addition to classification based on the presence or 
absence of provoking factors, various additional risk factors 
are considered to be involved in the risk of VTE recurrence. 
Representative factors include: age, sex, and obesity; 
thrombus location (PE or DVT); whether the event is an 
initial onset or a recurrence; and the presence of congenital 
thrombophilia. Furthermore, residual thrombus during 
the course of treatment and D-dimer levels after cessation 
of anticoagulation therapy may also influence the risk of 
recurrence. Several risk scoring systems that incorporate 
these additional factors and multiple other elements to 
predict VTE recurrence have been reported;
456 however, 
few have gained widespread use at present. Considerations 
regarding these additional factors are detailed below.
•

 and
 sex:

 evidence
 is
 inconsistent;

 studies

 ▋ 6.3  Determining the Duration of Therapy for PE 
Based on Recurrence Risk
Evidence regarding the optimal duration of anticoagulation 
for PE in Japan is limited, and guidance is based primarily 
on international studies. Current international guidelines 
classify patients into 5 risk groups and recommend treat-
ment durations accordingly
2,3,469,470 ( Table  15). For VTE 
caused by a transient major risk factor, 3-month anticoagu-
lation is generally sufficient. For VTE due to a transient 
minor risk factor, recommendations vary, with some 
suggesting 3 months is adequate
2,469 and others favoring 
longer durations depending on recurrence risk.3,470 Given 
the lower thrombosis risk and higher bleeding risk among 
Japanese patients, a limited duration (3 months) of 
anticoagulation therapy may be sufficient in the absence of 
additional risk factors. In PE patients with higher clinical 
severity, extended anticoagulation may be more strongly 
considered. Japanese data show that VTE due to minor 
transient risk factors has a significantly higher recurrence 
rate after discontinuation of anticoagulation than VTE 
due to major transient factors.
471 According to international 
guidelines, unprovoked VTE, VTE with persistent non-
cancer-related provoking factors, and VTE associated with 
active cancer are all considered to carry at least a moderate 
risk of recurrence. For these conditions, anticoagulation 
therapy longer than 3 months is recommended, with the 
aim of indefinite continuation. Japanese data have similarly 
shown that in patient groups with at least moderate recur-
rence risk, including unprovoked VTE, the recurrence risk 
after discontinuation of anticoagulation therapy was 
comparable to that reported in Western countries.
471 From 
the standpoint of recurrence risk alone, continued antico-
agulation therapy for as long as possible is recommended 
for patients with PE, in accordance with Western guidelines.
 ▋ 6.4 Bleeding Risks Associated With Anticoagulation
Anticoagulants carry a risk of bleeding. During warfarin 
therapy, the annual risk of major bleeding is slightly under 
3%.
472 In contrast, meta-analyses of phase III trials have 
shown that DOACs reduce the risk of major bleeding by 
approximately 40% compared with warfarin.
473 In East 
Asian populations, including Japanese patients, edoxaban 
therapy showed a 1.6% rate of major bleeding and a 9.9% 
rate of clinically relevant bleeding.
474 Observational studies 
during the warfarin era reported a 5.2% rate of major 
bleeding at 1 year, suggesting that real-world bleeding risks 
may be higher than in clinical trials.
451,475
The bleeding risk is especially high during the first month 
of therapy. Known risk factors include advanced age, prior 
bleeding, anemia, active cancer, prior stroke, chronic kidney 
disease, liver dysfunction, and concomitant antiplatelet 
therapy. Several bleeding risk scores have been developed. 
The VTE-BLEED score has been externally validated in 
Japanese populations and shown moderate utility, although 
it performs poorly in cancer patients.
476 Japanese data 
during the warfarin era identified active cancer (HR 3.06), 
prior major bleeding (HR 2.38), anemia (HR 1.75),

 ▋ 6.5  Treatment Duration Considering Both 
Recurrence and Bleeding Risks
Although no universally accepted strategy exists for deter-
mining the duration of anticoagulation therapy, many 
guidelines suggest evaluating recurrence and bleeding risks 
together. Based on the aforementioned recurrence risk, it 
has also been proposed that the duration of anticoagulation 
therapy should be determined with additional consideration 
of the bleeding risk (Figure
 
15).477 Because both can fluctuate 
over time, periodic reassessment is essential. It is also 
important to consider that the clinical consequences of PE 
and DVT differ, which may influence decisions regarding 
the duration of anticoagulation therapy.
 ▋ 6.6 Anticoagulation Therapy During Pregnancy
Warfarin crosses the placenta and is associated with 
embryopathy when used between Weeks 6–12 of gestation, 
and with central nervous system abnormalities or fetal 
bleeding when used later in pregnancy. Therefore, warfarin 
should be avoided during pregnancy. Among the DOACs, 
only rivaroxaban is explicitly contraindicated in pregnancy 
according to the package insert in Japan. For the other 
DOACs, the instructions for use state they should only be 
used when the therapeutic benefits are deemed to outweigh 
the risks. However, because the impact on the fetus and 
long-term outcomes in infants remain unknown, DOAC 
use during pregnancy is not currently recommended.
478 
Heparin, which has minimal placental transfer and no 
teratogenicity, is considered the only anticoagulant safe for 
long-term use during pregnancy. In Japan, LMWH is not 
covered by medical insurance, so unfractionated heparin is 
used via subcutaneous injection or continuous intravenous 
infusion during the perinatal period.
 ▋ 6.7 Anticoagulation Therapy During Breastfeeding
Warfarin has minimal transfer into breast milk and can be 
safely used during the lactation period. In contrast, all 
DOACs are known to transfer into breast milk, and breast-
feeding should generally be avoided while they are being 
used. That said, pharmacologic studies suggest that rivar-
oxaban transfers into breast milk in only small amounts.
479 
Moreover, a report on pharmacokinetics in a neonate 
breastfed by a mother taking rivaroxaban indicated that 
safe management was possible, suggesting that rivaroxaban 
may be a potential option under careful consideration.
480
7. Catheter-Directed Therapy for Acute VTE
Catheter-directed treatment for acute PE is broadly 
divided into 2 categories: with and without thrombolytic 
agents
481,482 (Recommendation Table  28; Table  16). When 
used with thrombolytics, the catheter tip is placed within 
the pulmonary arterial thrombus, and the thrombolytic

drug is administered directly into the clot. Compared with 
systemic administration via peripheral veins, this approach 
aims to achieve effective thrombolysis with smaller doses. 
It can be used in patients at high risk of bleeding but is 
contraindicated in those with active bleeding, especially 
intracranial hemorrhage.
Catheter-directed therapies without thrombolytics allow 
removal of pulmonary artery thrombi without increasing 
bleeding complications and are less invasive than surgical 
embolectomy, making them suitable for high-risk patients 
with active bleeding. Catheter-directed thrombolytic 
therapies include catheter-directed thrombolysis (CDT), 
delivery of thrombolytics to the thrombus site via a catheter, 
rheolytic thrombectomy (e.g., AngioJet
®; whereby throm-
bolytics soften the thrombus and the Venturi effect assists 
aspiration), and ultrasound-assisted thrombolysis (USAT; 
e.g., EkoSonic
TM; whereby ultrasound waves enhance 
thrombolysis).
In an RCT comparing CDT with systemic thrombolysis, 
no difference in thrombolytic efficacy was found unless the 
catheter penetrated the thrombus and the agent was 
administered directly into the thrombus.
483 In one study, 
USAT significantly improved the RV to LV ratio at 24
 h in
 
59 PE patients with right heart strain compared with 
heparin alone.
484 However, in another study of 81 patients 
with submassive PE, no significant difference in thrombus 
reduction (Miller score at 48
 h) was found between CDT
 
and USAT.485
Recently, catheter therapies without thrombolytics have 
shown favorable outcomes with fewer bleeding compli-
cations in intermediate- to high-risk PE patients.
486–488 
These therapies include the INARI FlowTriever® system, 
a large-bore flexible aspiration catheter with a thrombus 
removal disc and blood recovery system, and the Penumbra 
Indigo
® system, a medium-bore flexible catheter with a 
continuous, sustained suction pressure mechanism. These 
devices are not yet approved in Japan, and manual aspira-
tion using guiding catheters remains the standard. Reported 
complications include bleeding at the puncture site, pulmo-
nary hemorrhage, arrhythmia, hemolysis, hemoglobinuria, 
acute renal failure, right heart injury, and pulmonary 
artery injury.
The FLARE study using the INARI FlowTriever
® 
showed a 25.1% improvement in the RV/LV ratio at 48
 h
 
in 106 intermediate-risk PE patients, with a major bleeding 
rate of 1%.
488 The interim results of the FLASH study 
reported similar improvements (22.1% decrease in the 
RV/LV ratio) with a 1.2% major bleeding rate and a 0.4% 
30-day mortality rate in 250 high- and intermediate-risk 
patients.
487 In the EXTRACT-PE registry, the use of the 
Penumbra Indigo ® system in 119 intermediate-risk PE 
patients significantly improved the RV/LV ratio at 48
 h,
 
with a low rate of major complications.488
However, none of these catheter interventions has been 
validated through large RCTs against anticoagulation or 
systemic thrombolysis. As of March 2025, early approval 
applications have been submitted to the Japanese 
Government by related academic societies in Japan for 
unapproved catheter thrombectomy devices.
8. Surgical Embolectomy for PE  
(Recommendation Table
 
29)
High-risk PE carries a very high in-hospital mortality rate 
(25–65%).
489,490 Therefore, invasive treatments, including 
thrombolytic therapy and surgical interventions, may be 
required in addition to anticoagulation. Surgery is indicated 
in patients for whom thrombolytic therapy is contrain-
dicated due to bleeding risk, as well as those who do not 
respond to catheter-directed or thrombolytic therapies. 
Surgical pulmonary embolectomy has the advantage of 
rapidly reducing RV pressure overload and improving 
hemodynamics immediately after the procedure. It is 
especially useful in patients who cannot maintain circulatory 
stability with vasopressor support. In patients with free-
floating thrombi in the right atrium or ventricle, which 
carry a 29% mortality risk, surgery should be considered 
due to the potential for sudden hemodynamic collapse.
491
Although intermediate-risk PE generally has a low in-
hospital mortality rate and is not routinely an indication 
for surgery, intermediate- to high-risk patients with 
worsening hemodynamics, respiratory failure, or right heart 
dysfunction under anticoagulation or thrombolytic therapy 
may be considered surgical candidates. There are subgroups 
of intermediate- to high-risk PE patients with higher 
mortality, and severity scores such as PESI may assist with 
surgical decision-making.
492 For acute decompensation of
