chronic thromboembolic pulmonary hypertension (acute-
on-chronic PE), pulmonary endarterectomy under deep 
hypothermic circulatory arrest is required, and simple 
surgical embolectomy is not appropriate.
Reported 30-day mortality rates range from 2.3% to 19%, 
with rates of 0–23.7% for massive PE and 0–11.4% for 
submassive PE.
492–496 Preoperative cardiopulmonary resus-
citation is significantly associated with higher mortality.497,498 
The mortality rate for patients who failed thrombolytic 
therapy was 27%, compared with 3.6% for those who 
underwent surgery alone.
499 Massive PE is associated with 
more postoperative complications than submassive PE.494 
A meta-analysis showed that 33% of patients with acute PE 
required prolonged mechanical ventilation, 14% developed 
respiratory failure, and 13% developed pneumonia.
500 
VA-ECMO was used in 16% of patients.500 Re-exploration 
for bleeding was required in 8.4% of patients, and was 
more common in patients who had received preoperative 
thrombolysis.
494
9. IVC Filters
The fundamental principle in the prevention and treatment 
of acute PE is anticoagulation. IVC filters are medical 
devices used to supplement anticoagulant therapy.
501,502 
Regarding the indications for IVC filter placement in 
patients who can receive anticoagulation (i.e., relative 
indications), the PREPIC trial, which used permanent IVC 
filters, demonstrated a short-term benefit in preventing PE 
(4.8% vs. 1.1%).
503 However, this benefit was offset by a 
higher rate of DVT recurrence in the medium term (20.8% 
vs. 11.6%). A follow-up after 8 years yielded similar 
results.
504 In the PREPIC2 trial, which used retrievable filters 
(ALN filters), the filters were removed after 3 months. 505 
The incidence of PE at 3 and 6 months was 3.0% and 3.5% 
in the filter group, respectively, compared with 1.5% and 
2.0%, respectively, in the non-filter group. There were no 
significant differences in mortality. Based on these findings, 
the 10th edition of the American College of Chest Physicians 
guidelines (2016) recommends against IVC filter use in 
patients with acute VTE who can receive anticoagulation.
506 
However, in proximal DVT, where recurrence could be 
fatal, expert placement may be considered.
Common reasons for inadequate or unsuccessful antico-
agulation include poor adherence, subtherapeutic antico-
agulant levels, drug interactions, incorrect dosing, or 
anatomical abnormalities underlying VTE.
507 Most of 
these factors may be corrected. However, in patients with 
worsening or recurrent PE despite adequate anticoagu-
lation, IVC filter placement is considered a Class IIa 
recommendation (Recommendation Table
 
30).
Analysis of US national inpatient data showed that 
among unstable patients undergoing thrombolysis (those 
in shock or on mechanical ventilation), in-hospital mortality 
was significantly lower in the filter than non-filter group 
(7.6% vs. 18%).
508 The mortality benefit was especially 
notable in patients aged 80 years and older (RR 0.35).509 In 
a Japanese cohort study, patients receiving thrombolysis 
had a significantly reduced risk of in-hospital death when 
a filter was used (RR 0.51).
510 Thus, in patients undergoing 
intensive treatment such as thrombolysis or catheter-
directed therapy for acute PE or DVT, IVC filter placement 
may be considered to prevent worsening PE. A systematic 
review in the UK concluded that IVC filters have similar 
utility in pregnant as in non-pregnant patients.
511
Regarding prophylactic filter use in severely injured 
trauma patients without confirmed VTE, RCTs have 
shown no difference in PE incidence between those with 
and without filters.
512 Similarly, US inpatient data revealed 
a higher incidence of PE in trauma patients with fractures 
who received filters (14.7%) than in those who did not 
(0.5%).
506 This aligns with previous studies indicating that 
delayed or interrupted pharmacologic prophylaxis for ≥5 
days after trauma increases the risk of VTE (DVT, PE).513 
Therefore, in trauma patients without a confirmed diagnosis 
of VTE, routine IVC filter placement for primary prevention 
is not recommended (Class III recommendation).
Considering the complications of IVC filters, they should 
be removed promptly once they are no longer needed.

comparable to that of warfarin.
471,514
 ▋ 1.2 Distal DVT
Patients with distal DVT were excluded from major DOAC 
randomized controlled trials (RCTs), and therefore, the 
evidence base is limited.
447,515–517 Both international and 
Japanese guidelines refrain from recommending uniform 
anticoagulation for distal DVT.
469,506,518–520 Two RCTs have 
assessed low-molecular-weight heparin and compression 
therapy for symptomatic distal DVT without cancer. 
Although initial exploratory studies showed benefit, later 
trials showed no efficacy for anticoagulation and increased 
bleeding risk.
521,522
In symptomatic distal DVT, if no proximal propagation 
is observed within 2 weeks, further extension is unlikely.518,523 
In Japan, asymptomatic distal DVT generally has a favorable 
natural course,
524 with better outcomes than symptomatic 
DVT.
In Japanese clinical practice, screening is commonly 
conducted for safety purposes, and nearly half of all cases 
of detected distal DVT are asymptomatic.
525 Recent 
screening among cancer patients showed a 5.9% prevalence 
of venous thromboembolism (VTE), with 99.7% of patients 
being asymptomatic and 78.8% having distal DVT.
526 In 
the screening of DVT in a general healthy population, the 
detection rate for distal DVT was 4.3%.
527 In contrast, 
symptomatic PE is rare (3.1 cases per 10,000 perioperative 
patients),
528 and hospital data indicate 124.2 PE cases per 
100,000 inpatients.529 Despite the screening of DVT being 
performed in only a limited number of hospitals, the overall 
incidence of PE remains low, suggesting that not all distal 
DVTs pose significant risk.
For patients with distal DVT considered at low risk of 
recurrence, only follow-up ultrasonography at 7–14 
days is recommended. Anticoagulation should be reserved 
for patients with proximal propagation or high-risk 
features.
506,518,519
Features of high-risk distal DVT include: symptomatic 
presentation not detected by screening; large, fresh thrombi 
near the popliteal vein; active cancer; a history of VTE; 
and perioperative status or hospitalization.
518 Considering 
the risks of bleeding and healthcare costs, routine antico-
agulation for all cases of distal DVT should be avoided. 
Instead, the following approach is recommended:
PM
  Monitor with ultrasonography at 7–14 days without
 
anticoagulation.
2.
  Administer anticoagulation only to patients with high
 
recurrence risk.
For asymptomatic distal DVT detected by screening or 
incidental imaging, follow-up with ultrasonography and 
physical therapy is more appropriate. In contrast, antico-
agulation may be considered in patients with symptoms, a 
history of VTE, large thrombi, those undergoing orthopedic 
or cancer surgery, or those receiving chemotherapy.
530 
Given the low recurrence rate of distal DVT, a 3-month 
treatment period is generally appropriate.
464,531,532
2. Catheter-Directed Therapy for Acute DVT  
and Post-Thrombotic Syndrome  
(Recommendation Table
 
28)
 ▋ 2.1 Acute DVT
For extensive iliofemoral DVT in the acute phase, catheter-
directed therapies include catheter-directed thrombolysis 
(CDT) as used for PE;
533 percutaneous mechanical throm-
bectomy, which involves thrombus fragmentation and 
aspiration without thrombolytics; and pharmacomechanical 
CDT (PCDT), which is a combination of CDT and 
percutaneous mechanical thrombectomy.
534
RCTs have assessed the efficacy of these therapies. The 
CaVenT trial compared CDT with conventional antico-
agulation in patients with iliofemoral DVT and found 
superior vein patency at 6 months and a lower rate of post-
thrombotic syndrome (PTS) at 24 months.
535 The difference 
in PTS incidence was even greater at 5 years.536
The ATTRACT trial, comparing PCDT to anticoagu-
lation in 692 patients with proximal DVT, showed no 
difference in overall PTS or VTE recurrence, but PCDT 
reduced the incidence of severe PTS
537 Although PCDT 
increased major bleeding, subgroup analysis of iliofemoral 
DVT showed that PCDT alleviated early leg pain and 
swelling, reduced PTS severity, and improved quality of 
life.
538
In the CAVA trial, involving 184 patients with iliofemoral 
DVT, PTS incidence and quality of life at 1 year were 
comparable between ultrasound-assisted CDT and antico-
agulation alone.
539
Based on these RCTs, Western guidelines (e.g., American 
College of Chest Physicians, European Society of Cardiology) 
recommend anticoagulation as the primary treatment for 
DVT.
3,469,470 Endovascular treatment should be considered 
for young patients at low bleeding risk with severely 
symptomatic iliofemoral DVT or phlegmasia cerulea dolens.
During CDT, unfractionated heparin is administered to 
prevent thrombus extension and recurrence. Placement of 
an inferior vena cava filter during CDT may help prevent 
PE from embolized thrombi but does not improve long-
term outcomes.
540,541 If an inferior vena cava filter is applied, 
retrievable filters are recommended, and removal should 
be attempted once the embolic risk diminishes.
542
For residual stenosis after thrombolysis (e.g., May–
Thurner syndrome), balloon angioplasty or stenting can 
help maintain venous outflow, reduce recurrence, and 
improve outcomes.
543,544 However, high rates of in-stent 
restenosis have also been reported.545
In Japan, urokinase had been the only thrombolytic agent 
approved for DVT, but its supply was suspended during 
the COVID-19 pandemic due to loss of urine collection for 
production. This led to the approval of 2 large-bore 
thrombectomy devices that do not require thrombolytics: 
Indigo
® (Penumbra) and ClotTriever ® (Inari Medical). 
Early results have been favorable, but no comparative data 
with anticoagulation are available, and the indication for 
these devices as of March 2025 remains limited to selected 
severe cases.
546–548 In particular, phlegmasia cerulea dolens 
accompanied by arterial ischemia, although rare, carries a 
risk of limb amputation. Surgical venous thrombectomy 
has conventionally been the mainstay of treatment; however, 
thrombectomy devices that enable early reperfusion with 
less invasiveness are expected to be useful.
549 According to 
Advance Publication
[[p56]]
56
TAMURA Y et al.
the appropriate use criteria for thrombectomy devices in 
Japan,482 patient indications include severe DVT involving 
acute-phase symptoms such as iliac vein obstruction 
accompanied by arterial ischemia (e.g., phlegmasia cerulea 
dolens, phlegmasia alba dolens, or venous gangrene) or 
DVT that causes persistent pain despite adequate conser-
vative therapy (e.g., anticoagulation, limb elevation, or 
compression therapy) and significantly impairs daily 
activities (defined as a Revised Venous Clinical Severity 
Score pain score of 3, or a pain score of 2 combined with a 
Villalta score of ≥15).
 ▋ 2.2 Post-Thrombotic Syndrome
PTS refers to a condition where chronic venous stasis 
symptoms and findings appear due to residual thrombus 
after DVT or regurgitation caused by valve destruction, 
ultimately presenting as refractory venous leg ulcers. 
Although many cases of PTS improve with lifestyle guidance 
and compression therapy, such as elastic stockings, some 
patients experience recurrent venous leg ulcers or persistent 
pain. Catheter-directed treatments, including venous stenting, 
have demonstrated efficacy for these PTS patients.
550–552 
However, there are no RCTs comparing catheter-directed 
treatments to conservative therapy, and they should be 
reserved for severe cases where conservative therapy has 
failed. Treatment must follow appropriate use criteria of 
venous stents.
482
3. Surgical Thrombectomy for DVT 
(Recommendation Table
 
32)
Most cases of DVT are treated with anticoagulation alone. 
However, in severe cases, endovascular therapies may be 
introduced, and surgical thrombectomy combined with 
endovascular intervention is performed in select patients.
553 
Surgical thrombectomy is indicated only for severe cases 
such as phlegmasia cerulea dolens, venous gangrene, and 
acute DVT with severe, persistent pain. Among patients 
with phlegmasia cerulea dolens, surgery is preferred in 
more severe cases because endovascular thrombolysis 
cannot deliver immediate relief.
549,554 Surgery also suitable 
for patients with contraindications to anticoagulation or 
thrombolysis due to high bleeding risk. Although surgical 
thrombectomy may help prevent PTS even in patients with 
mild DVT, the procedure is highly invasive.
555,556
In cases not limited to phlegmasia cerulea dolens, hybrid 
surgical thrombectomy with endovascular therapy resulted 
in a patency rate of 70–80% and a PTS incidence of 
10–20%.
557 Some reports indicate a lower PTS rate following 
surgery compared with anticoagulation alone.556 On analysis 
reported in 2008 amputation and mortality rates of 22% in 
phlegmasia cerulea dolens.
549 However, a more recent 
series of 15 patients reported no major amputations or 
deaths following surgical venous thrombectomy.
554
4. Physical Therapy for DVT  
(Recommendation Table
 
33)
 ▋ 4.1 Bed Rest and Mobilization
Historically, during the acute phase of DVT, strict bed rest 
was commonly prescribed due to concerns that mobili-
zation, including ambulation, could dislodge thrombi and 
trigger PE. However, recent studies have demonstrated that 
with appropriate anticoagulation therapy, early mobili-
zation does not increase the risk of new PE or thrombus 
propagation. In fact, early ambulation may improve leg 
pain.
558–560 If there is no floating thrombus, the patient has 
mild leg pain, and the general condition is stable, early 
mobilization and walking are recommended instead of bed 
rest because this may contribute to improved quality of 
life. Conversely, if floating thrombi are present, even with 
anticoagulation therapy, PE risk may still be high, requiring 
cautious clinical judgment.
561 In Japan, studies using the 
JROAD-DPC database show that the average hospital stay 
for DVT patients decreased from 14 days in 2012 to 12 days 
in 2017, coinciding with the increased use of DOACs.
562 
These agents likely contributed to earlier discharge and 
outpatient management.

Evidence for Surgical Treatment of DVT
COR LOE
 For severe acute DVT accompanied by arterial
 
ischemia, surgical venous thrombectomy 
should be considered
IIa C
COR, Class of Recommendation; DVT, deep vein thrombosis;

 ▋ 4.2 Compression Therapy (Acute Phase)
Compression therapy is initiated after starting anticoagu-
lation in the acute phase of DVT to relieve symptoms such 
as swelling and pain, and to prevent long-term compli-
cations like PTS. Theoretical benefits of compression 
therapy include an enhanced muscle pump effect through 
increased venous pressure during contraction, improved 
capillary perfusion and reduced edema through enhanced 
microcirculation, and reduced venous reflux by narrowing 
the vein diameter and promoting valve closure.
In Japan, approximately 70% of VTE patients receive 
compression therapy,
563 as do nearly half of DVT patients 
globally.564 Elastic stockings are preferred over elastic 
bandages due to ease of use and cost-effectiveness. There 
???????QT??
35
?g??K?TUT??QT??g????
chosen because of better adherence.566,567
Studies have shown that combining early ambulation 
with compression therapy under anticoagulation improves 
acute symptoms (e.g., pain and swelling) without increasing 
PE risk.
568–570 Compression may also promote thrombus 
recanalization and reduce the incidence of chronic venous 
obstruction, which leads to PTS. Although some studies 
question the effectiveness of compression for acute pain 
relief,
571 discrepancies may be due to poor adherence or 
inconsistent implementation of compression.570
 ▋ 4.3 Compression Therapy (Chronic Phase)
Small studies have suggested that wearing elastic stockings 
with an ankle pressure of ≥30
 mmH
g for 2 years can prevent 
PTS572–574 However, a large RCT comparing stockings to 
placebo showed no significant difference in PTS incidence.575 
Limitations of that study included poor adherence (only 
56%) and a lack of compression therapy during the acute 
phase. In an RCT comparing 1- vs. 2-year stocking use 
among patients with good adherence, non-inferiority of 
the 1-year group was not demonstrated, and 2-year wear 
was recommended.
576 For patients without PTS symptoms 
6 months after DVT onset (Villalta score), no difference in 
PTS incidence was found between 6-month and 2-year 
stocking use, suggesting therapy duration can be indi-
vidualized based on symptom severity.
577 Systematic 
reviews and meta-analyses have demonstrated the efficacy 
of compression therapy in preventing PTS, particularly in 
proximal DVT.
578
Elastic stockings rarely cause serious complications and 
are effective for symptom control in DVT and PTS. If 
swelling or pain improves, discontinuing use is reasonable. 
A practical approach is to apply compression stockings 
during the acute phase and to continue for 6 months. If 
PTS symptoms remain, continue for up to 2 years.
For patients with established PTS, the effectiveness of 
compression stockings in mild cases of PTS is uncertain.
579 
In the case of venous leg ulcers, compression bandages 
or stockings with ≥ 30
 mmHg ankle pressu
re are highly 
recommended.578–583 In patients with stasis dermatitis or 
lipodermatosclerosis, compression therapy is also recom-
mended despite low-level evidence.
578 Compression therapy 
also prevents ulcer recurrence, as supported by RCTs and 
systematic reviews.
578,584
High-pressure stockings are more difficult to wear and 
may reduce adherence. Thus, stockings with 20–30
 mmHg
 
pressure are recommended as in the acute phase. 565,578 
Therapeutic compression usually starts from >18
 mmHg
 
for symptom relief. For ulcers, pressures ≥ 40
 
mmHg are 
ideal, although lower pressures have also been shown to be 
effective.
583,585,586 Knee-length stockings are generally 
preferred over thigh-high stockings unless there is significant 
thigh edema, because the knee-length stockings are cheaper, 
easier to wear, and similarly effective.
587
X. Cancer-Associated Venous Thromboembolism
PM?d??bL`??
Venous Thromboembolism
It has long been recognized that the incidence of venous 
thromboembolism (VTE) is higher in cancer patients than 
in the general population. VTE is not only a serious 
complication during cancer treatment but is also associated 
with worsened survival. The incidence of VTE has shown 
an increasing trend in recent years, with the highest rates 
of VTE reported in pancreatic, gastric, and primary brain 
tumors.
588,589 Regarding racial differences, prospective 
epidemiological studies in the US have reported that the 
incidence of cancer-associated VTE in adults of Asian 
descent is lower than in other ethnic groups.
590 In Japan, 
the incidence of VTE in patients with solid tumors has 
been reported to be between 3.3% and 5.9%.
591,592 However, 
many of these cases are asymptomatic distal deep vein 
thrombosis (DVT), suggesting a high frequency of incidental 
findings. The incidence of VTE in Japanese patients with 
pancreatic cancer is also high, and risk factors include 
obesity (body mass index >25
 kg/m2) and administration of 
chemotherapy.593 However, due to the poor prognosis of 
pancreatic cancer itself, it has been reported that the occur-
rence of VTE or intervention with anticoagulant therapy 
does not contribute to improved survival in such cases.
2. Definition of Active Cancer
Active cancer is defined by the presence of any of the 
following conditions:
515 newly diagnosed cancer within 6 
months prior to enrollment; cancer treatment (surgery, 
chemotherapy, radiation therapy) within the past 6 months; 
ongoing cancer treatment (surgery, chemotherapy, radiation 
therapy); recurrent, locally invasive, or metastatic disease; 
and, in hematologic malignancies, the absence of complete 
remission.
Active cancer is considered a risk factor for the devel-
opment of VTE. Conversely, it has been demonstrated that 
patients with a history of cancer that is now cured do not 
have a high recurrence risk of VTE, and that their risk is 
comparable to that of non-cancer patients.
594 Therefore, 
decisions as to whether to continue anticoagulation should 
be based on the activity of the cancer rather than on the 
Advance Publication
[[p58]]
58
TAMURA Y et al.
mere history of malignancy.
3. Biomarkers and Risk Assessment
A prospective observational study evaluating D-dimer levels 
in cancer patients reported a cut-off value of 6.8
 μg/mL,
 
with a sensitivity of 96% and specificity of 38% for 
diagnosing VTE.
478 Meta-analyses have shown that the 
odds ratio for VTE in patients with elevated D-dimer levels 
vs. those without is between 1.85 and 1.92.
595,596 However, 
D-dimer levels vary widely among cancer patients depending 
on the underlying disease state. There are currently no 
coagulation or fibrinolysis biomarkers with sufficient 
evidence to replace D-dimer. Thus, in the diagnosis of VTE 
in cancer patients, scoring systems such as the Khorana 
score
597 (proposed by Khorana et al. in 2008) and the Vienna 
score598 (which incorporates D-dimer and soluble P-selectin) 
are commonly used.
Regarding the utility of the Khorana score in Japanese 
patients, the reported VTE prevalence according to risk 
categories is as follows: low risk (0 points), 1.7%; interme-
diate risk (1–2 points), 7.3%; and high risk (≥ 3 points), 
11.0%.
599 It is of note that these prevalence values are 
higher than the original report (low, 0.3%; intermediate, 
2%; high, 7%), particularly among intermediate-risk 
patients.
4. Treatment Algorithm
Treatment of cancer-associated VTE should be considered 
in 3 phases: initial treatment (first 7–10 days), long-term 
treatment (3–6 months), and extended treatment. In principle, 
direct oral anticoagulants (DOACs) are administered in 
the same dosages and regimens as for VTE unrelated to 
cancer.
For distal-type DVT frequently observed in cancer 
patients in Japan, there are no relevant international 
treatment data. The ONCO DVT study, a randomized 
controlled trial assessing the duration of edoxaban therapy 
in cancer-associated distal DVT, was recently conducted in 
Japan.
600 In that study, the 12-month treatment group 
showed fewer VTE events (mainly distal DVT) than the 
3-month group, with no significant difference in bleeding 
risk.
In real-world practice in Japan, analysis of health 
insurance claims data has shown that the introduction of 
DOACs has significantly increased the proportion of cancer-
associated VTE patients managed in outpatient settings. 
There was no marked difference in drug selection compared 
with non-cancer-associated VTE.
601 However, the use of 
initial intensified DOAC therapy as recommended interna-
tionally is relatively low, and approximately 80% of patients 
diagnosed with pulmonary embolism received anticoagu-
lation in inpatient settings.
Because of the high recurrence risk of cancer-associated 
VTE, international guidelines recommend continuing 
anticoagulation therapy for at least 6 months during periods 
of active cancer.
602 Japanese epidemiological data also 
confirm a high recurrence rate in patients with active cancer, 
supporting the need for extended treatment.
594 Conversely, 
in patients with a history of cancer that is no longer active, 
the recurrence risk is comparable to that of non-cancer 
patients, and treatment discontinuation may be considered 
once cancer activity resolves.
603
Cancer patients are generally at high risk of bleeding as 
well, making it difficult to balance the thrombotic and 
hemorrhagic risks. Therefore, the duration of anticoagu-
lation must be carefully individualized based on cancer 
status and patient-specific factors.
For many distal DVTs, a treatment period of 3 months 
is considered appropriate due to the generally low 
recurrence risk.
464,531 However, international reports have 
indicated high recurrence rates for distal DVT in patients 
with cancer, and anticoagulation has shown efficacy even 
in asymptomatic DVT patients.
604–606 Findings from the 
ONCO DVT study and subsequent subgroup analyses 
suggest that extended anticoagulation is particularly beneficial 
in patients with active cancer who are at high thrombotic 
risk but low bleeding risk.
532,600
Conversely, the efficacy of anticoagulation in all distal 
DVTs has not been fully established. Moreover, among 
cancer patients who underwent anticoagulation following 
screening, major bleeding was identified as a significant 
risk factor for mortality.
451 As of March 2025, no large-
scale systematic screening programs for cancer-associated 
VTE have shown a clear benefit of anticoagulation in all 
distal DVTs.
607
Advance Publication
[[p59]]
59
JCS/JPCPHS 2025 Guideline on PH and VTE
XI. Progression to Chronic Conditions (Post-Thrombotic Syndrome and  
Chronic Thromboembolic Pulmonary Disease) (Recommendation Table
 
34)
PM?oLs?r
Post-thrombotic syndrome (PTS) is a chronic condition 
resulting from residual thrombus and venous valve 
destruction following deep vein thrombosis (DVT). PTS is 
characterized by chronic symptoms and signs of venous 
stasis, including intractable venous leg ulcers in severe 
cases.
608–610
 ▋ 1.1 Epidemiology of and Risk Factors for PTS
PTS occurs in approximately 20–80% of patients after acute 
DVT, including mild cases.
611–614 A Japanese observational 
study reported a 13% incidence of PTS within 3 years after 
DVT.
615 Risk factors include extensive thrombus burden,616 
recurrent ipsilateral DVT,608,616,617 and the combined presence 
of deep venous occlusion and valve incompetence. 616,618 
Obesity, lifestyle factors, and superficial/perforating vein 
insufficiency also contribute. However, early and appro-
priate anticoagulation significantly reduces the incidence 
of PTS.
612,619,620
 ▋ 1.2 Diagnosis of PTS
A diagnosis of PTS is made using the Villalta score, which 
quantifies symptoms and physical signs. A Villalta score ≥5 
indicates mild PTS, a score of ≥10 indicates moderate PTS, 
and a score of ≥15 or the presence of venous leg ulcers 
indicates severe PTS.
621
 ▋ 1.3 Prevention of PTS
Prevention strategies for PTS include proper anticoagu-
lation therapy, the use of compression stockings after DVT 
onset, and early thrombus removal in select patients.
 ▋ 1.4 Treatment of PTS
PTS is treated similarly to chronic venous insufficiency. 
This includes lifestyle modification (e.g., reducing the 
duration of standing or dependent leg positions, weight 
loss), compression therapy with elastic stockings, and 
catheter-directed intervention for patients with severe 
symptoms, such as recurrent ulcers or pain associated with 
iliac vein lesions. In such patients, the usefulness of catheter-
directed therapies, including venous stenting, has been 
demonstrated.
550–552 However, no randomized controlled 
trials have compared these interventions with conservative 
therapy, and the procedure should be reserved only for 
severe cases where conservative measures have proven 
ineffective. Treatment should be performed in accordance 
with the appropriate use criteria for venous stent.
482
2. Chronic Thromboembolic  
Pulmonary Disease
Some patients experience persistent exertional dyspnea, 
reduced exercise tolerance, and decreased quality of life 
after acute pulmonary embolism (PE), a condition referred 
to as post-PE syndrome.
622–624 Although the pathogenesis 
and clinical spectrum are not fully understood, patients 
who have persistent symptoms due to organized thrombi 
in the pulmonary arteries are diagnosed with chronic 
thromboembolic pulmonary disease (CTEPD), regardless 
of the presence of pulmonary hypertension. If pulmonary 
hypertension is present, the condition is termed chronic 
thromboembolic pulmonary hypertension (CTEPH).
7
At the time of acute PE diagnosis, it is crucial to start 
anticoagulation therapy promptly to prevent residual 
thrombus and the development of CTEPD. A follow-up 
clinical evaluation at 3–6 months after acute PE is recom-
mended to assess for recurrence, bleeding, malignancy, 
ongoing exercise limitation, or new symptoms that may 
warrant extended anticoagulation therapy.
441,448,449,625 For 
patients at high risk of developing CTEPH, or those 
presenting with dyspnea or signs of right heart strain at 
follow-up, active evaluation for residual thrombus should 
be considered.
2
Compared with lung perfusion scintigraphy, computed 
tomography (CT) has a lower negative predictive value for 
diagnosing CTEPH. Therefore, lung perfusion scintigraphy 
is preferred for evaluating residual thrombus.
22,626 Where 
nuclear medicine examination is unavailable, contrast-
enhanced CT with appropriate protocols or dual-energy

Given the current lack of strong evidence in VTE 
prevention in Japan, mechanical prophylaxis is emphasized 
over pharmacologic approaches because of bleeding compli-
cations with pharmacologic prophylaxis. For the highest-
risk patients, anticoagulant therapy is recommended, but 
if the bleeding risk is high, mechanical prophylaxis alone 
may be considered. The risk level associated with each 
surgery or condition should be assessed comprehensively, 
including additional risk factors (Table
 
19). Depending 
on these risk factors, a higher level of prevention may be 
considered.
In many facilities, mechanical prophylaxis such as elastic 
stockings or intermittent pneumatic compression (IPC), 
which are associated with fewer adverse events, are widely 
used for patients at low to high risk. For the highest-risk 
patients (e.g., those with a history of VTE or thrombophilia), 
anticoagulant therapy is added. Since the introduction of 
reimbursement for pulmonary embolism (PE) prevention 
management, the increased use of mechanical prophylaxis 
and early mobilization, and greater patient awareness of 
PE, the incidence of perioperative PE has decreased by 
approximately half.
528
For other perioperative VTE risk assessments, the 
Caprini score is the most widely studied.629,630 However, it 
has not been broadly adopted in Japan due to its complexity, 
lack of validation, and inconsistent application.
631 In some 
Asian countries, studies with stricter definitions of high 
risk have been conducted.
629,632
For medical patients, VTE risk can be assessed using 
the Padua score, 633 or the IMPROVE VTE risk score, 634 
whereas the Khorana score 597,635 is used for outpatients 
with cancer. However, these tools have limited external 
validation in Japan, and are not widely implemented.
636,637 
Recently, the COMPASS-CAT score, which offers higher 
precision for cancer patients, has also been reported.
638
In Japan, screening tests are performed in some institu-
tions to detect high-risk patients. Among cancer surgery 
patients, preoperative D-dimer testing and lower extremity

639 Although some studies have 
demonstrated the clinical utility of screening tests, others 
have reported no benefit. Due to cost-effectiveness concerns,

607
There is no universally accepted tool for predicting the 
bleeding risk of pharmacologic prophylaxis. Although the 
American College of Clinical Pharmacy (ACCP) guidelines 
list bleeding risk factors
640 and scoring systems (such as the

While bleeding risk is also a concern during therapeutic 
anticoagulation, it is also particularly important during 
pharmacologic prophylaxis.
2. Methods of VTE Prevention
 ▋ 2.1 Early Ambulation and Active Exercise
Early mobilization and active limb exercise are fundamental 
to VTE prevention. Encouraging ambulation activates the 
calf muscle pump and reduces venous stasis in the lower 
limbs.
643 For patients unable to ambulate, leg elevation, 
massage, and passive or active ankle exercises are recom-
mended.
644–646 These methods are safe, free, and should be 
implemented in all patients unless contraindicated.
 ▋ 2.2 Elastic Stockings
Elastic stockings reduce the total cross-sectional area of 
the veins by compressing the lower limbs, thereby increasing 
venous blood flow and reducing stasis.
647,648 Compared 
with other preventive methods, stockings have the advan-
tages of no bleeding risk, simplicity, and relatively low 
cost. They are effective in moderate-risk patients, but less 
so when used alone in high-risk or highest-risk patients.
649 
Much of the evidence for elastic stockings is based on older 
studies and focuses on asymptomatic VTE as an outcome, 
which is why the ACCP guidelines assign them a lower 
recommendation grade.
650 However, in Japan, early 
mobilization combined with elastic stockings, initiated for 
the prevention of VTE in 2004, has been associated with a 
near halving of the incidence of perioperative PE.
528 Given 
the safety profile of elastic stockings, their affordability, 
and their ability to raise VTE awareness among patients 
and healthcare providers, continuous use before, during, 
and after surgery is recommended in surgical patients at 
risk. The risks associated with elastic stockings are the 
same as those described for therapeutic compression. For 
more detailed recommendations, refer to the Compression 
Therapy Guidelines for Venous Diseases 2025.
578
 ▋ 2.3 Intermittent Pneumatic Compression
IPC involves periodically inflating cuffs wrapped around 
the legs to reduce venous stasis, similar to elastic stockings. 
It is effective even in high-risk patients and is particularly 
beneficial when bleeding risk precludes pharmacologic 
prophylaxis.
651,652 Combined use with pharmacologic 
prophylaxis further enhances preventive efficacy.653 Meta-
analyses have shown that IPC significantly reduces the 
incidence of PE and DVT compared with no prophylaxis. 
Compared with elastic stockings alone, IPC more effectively 
reduces DVT, although there is no significant difference for 
PE. Compared with pharmacologic prophylaxis, IPC 
shows similar effects in preventing PE, DVT, and death, 
but with less bleeding risk.
653 Recent studies have shown 
that IPC significantly reduces the incidence of proximal 
DVT in stroke patients with lower-limb paralysis.
654 These 
findings have led to renewed interest in IPC use for stroke 
rehabilitation. However, evidence as to the efficacy of IPC 
in long-term paralysis is limited. The ACCP guidelines 
recommend IPC alone for high-risk patients (e.g., postop-
erative surgical or orthopedic patients) when bleeding risk 
is high.
640,650
 ▋ 2.4 Low-Dose Unfractionated Heparin
For the prevention of VTE using unfractionated heparin, 
subcutaneous administration of 5,000 units every 8 or 12
 h
 
is common. In postoperative VTE prophylaxis, heparin 
calcium preparations are used, and a standard regimen 
involves subcutaneous administration of 5,000 units every 
12
 h for 7–10 days. Notably, this dosage does not prolong
 
activated partial thromboplastin time (APTT), and unlike 
low-molecular-weight heparin (LMWH), it is subject to 
fewer medical insurance restrictions regarding timing of 
administration and underlying disease conditions, thus 
allowing for broad use in prophylaxis. Although unfrac-
tionated heparin may be sufficiently effective as mono-
therapy in high-risk patients, in the highest-risk patients 
combined use with mechanical prophylaxis is recom-
mended.
655,656 Therapy should be continued at least until the 
patient can walk adequately, and, if long-term prevention 
is necessary, switching to warfarin should be considered. It 
is important to note that with use beyond 5 days, the risk 
of heparin-induced thrombocytopenia increases; thus the 
platelet count should be monitored during extended use. 
The timing of initiation depends on individual risk, but this 
method does not require monitoring, is relatively inex-
pensive, and has a favorable safety profile. However, evalu-
ation of bleeding risk is essential, particularly in relation to 
spinal or epidural anesthesia. Indeed, there are reports 
indicating that subcutaneous administration of 2,500 units 
of unfractionated heparin every 12
 h effectively prevented
 
VTE without increasing hemorrhagic complications in 
patients undergoing spinal or epidural anesthesia.
657
 ▋ 2.5 Dose-Adjusted Unfractionated Heparin
This is a method that enhances efficacy by adjusting APTT 
to the upper limit of the normal range. An initial subcu-
taneous injection of approximately 3,500 units of unfrac-
tionated heparin is administered, followed by subcutaneous 
injections every 8
 h at a dose adjusted by 
±500 units based 
on the APTT measured 4
 h after administration to reach
 
the target range. In some cases, continuous intravenous 
infusion may be used. Although this method is complex, it 
can be effective as monotherapy in patients at the highest 
risk.
658 However, careful attention must be paid to hemor-
rhagic complications.
 ▋ 2.6 Dose-Adjusted Warfarin
This method involves the oral administration of warfarin 
with dose adjustment to achieve a target international 
normalized ratio of prothrombin time (PT-INR); however, 
it is generally not implemented as a method of VTE 
prophylaxis. Because it takes 3–5 days for the effects of oral 
warfarin to manifest, it should either be initiated preopera-
Advance Publication
[[p63]]
63
JCS/JPCPHS 2025 Guideline on PH and VTE
tively or be combined with another prophylactic method 
during the initial phase of administration. Although a 
PT-INR range of 2.0–3.0 is recommended in Western 
countries, a PT-INR range of 1.5–2.5 is considered 
appropriate under current conditions in Japan. Although 
the need for monitoring is a disadvantage, warfarin is 
effective even as monotherapy in patients at the highest 
risk and has the advantages of being inexpensive and orally 
administrable.
659
 ▋ 2.7 LMWH and Factor Xa Inhibitors
New anticoagulants such as LMWH, fondaparinux, and 
edoxaban exhibit minimal interindividual variability in 
effect and can be conveniently administered by subcutaneous 
injection once or twice daily, or orally, without the need 
for monitoring. In addition, the incidence of adverse 
effects, such as heparin-induced thrombocytopenia and 
elevations in liver enzymes, is low. Consequently, LMWH 
has become the mainstay of both treatment and prevention 
of VTE in Western countries.
640,650 Among the LMWHs 
used for VTE treatment abroad, enoxaparin is covered by 
medical insurance in Japan exclusively for VTE prophylaxis. 
Enoxaparin is approved for use following total hip arthro-
plasty, total knee arthroplasty, and hip fracture surgery, as 
well as in postoperative settings of abdominal surgery with 
a high risk of VTE.
660 Administration of 20  mg (2,000 
units) enoxaparin twice daily reduced the incidence of 
asymptomatic DVT after total knee arthroplasty from 
60.8% to 29.8%, without a significant increase in hemor
-
rhagic complications.661 In Japan, the Factor Xa inhibitors 
approved for medical insurance coverage in the prevention 
of VTE in surgical patients are the subcutaneous injection 
formulation fondaparinux and the oral agent edoxaban. 
Fondaparinux is approved for the prevention of VTE in 
patients undergoing lower limb orthopedic surgery and 
abdominal surgery who are at high risk of VTE.
662–664
3. Assessment and Prevention of VTE in  
Internal Medicine (Non-Surgical) Inpatients  
(Recommendation Table
 
36)
An analysis by the Japan Medical Safety Research 
Organization on fatalities related to acute PE found that 4 
of 8 deaths occurred in non-surgical, non-trauma patients 
(e.g., due to cerebral hemorrhage, psychiatric illness, 
pleuritis).
665 In addition, a national epidemiological study 
using Diagnosis Procedure Combination data reported that, 
compared with surgical patients, medical inpatients had 
lower implementation rates of VTE prevention (7.3% vs. 
59.2%) and a higher PE incidence (0.119% vs. 0.095%).
666 
These findings underscore the importance of PE risk 
management in internal medicine patients as well.
In medical inpatients, reduced physical activity, venous 
stasis, hypercoagulability, and endothelial dysfunction all 
increase VTE risk. The total VTE risk level should be 
assessed by integrating these factors
633,634,667,668 while also 
considering bleeding risk and complications such as skin 
injury or ischemic necrosis associated with the use of 
elastic stockings. Preventive strategies should be selected 
accordingly.
As of March 2025, unfractionated heparin is the only 
anticoagulant approved in Japan for primary VTE preven-
tion in medical inpatients, whereas LMWH, fondaparinux, 
and DOACs are used internationally.
The information in this section is primarily targeted to 
bedridden acute-phase medical patients (e.g., bed rest

with bathroom privileges). Unless contraindicated, early 
mobilization and active exercise are recommended in all 
disease states. In the acute phase of stroke, IPC is preferred 
over elastic stockings in patients who cannot walk 
independently.
654,669,670 Prophylactic anticoagulation may 
be considered in patients at high VTE risk, but only if the 
expected benefit outweighs the risk of intra- or extracranial 
hemorrhage. Currently, no predictive tools exist to reliably 
identify such patients.
671 In cases where antithrombotic 
therapy is already being used for stroke (e.g., ischemic 
stroke), adding prophylactic anticoagulation increases the 
risk of hemorrhagic transformation or brain hemorrhage.
670 
Therefore, its efficacy has not been established. In severe 
hemorrhagic stroke, subarachnoid hemorrhage, or hemor-
rhagic infarction, non-pharmacologic methods should be 
prioritized.
670
For acute myocardial infarction, one small randomized 
controlled trial (RCT) confirmed that the use of elastic 
stockings reduces the incidence of DVT.
672 In cancer 
patients, especially those with active malignancy and hospi-
talized for acute illness, or outpatients with limited mobility 
undergoing chemotherapy (e.g., Khorana score ≥ 2), 
prophylactic anticoagulation with LMWH or direct oral 
anticoagulants has been shown to be effective in international 
studies.
602,673–683 However, these agents are not approved 
for this indication in Japan, and neither safety data nor 
domestic evidence are available. Given the high bleeding 
risk in cancer patients, careful risk–benefit analysis is 
essential. Therefore, such prophylaxis should be limited to 
high-risk cancer patients.
In other internal medicine conditions, such as congestive 
heart failure, acute infections, chronic obstructive pulmo-
nary disease exacerbations, inflammatory disorders, and 
inflammatory bowel disease, the risk of VTE increases with 
prolonged immobility (≥3–4 days) and coexisting factors 
such as a history of VTE, obesity, hormone therapy, 
advanced age, malignancy, or varicose veins. In these cases, 
prophylactic anticoagulation with LMWH or fondaparinux 
has demonstrated efficacy internationally.
682–684
VTE risk remains elevated for up to 6 weeks after 
discharge in high-risk medical patients.634,685–687 Short-term 
(∼40 days) prophylaxis has been shown to reduce this risk, 
but the risk of major bleeding also increases.688–691 Therefore, 
extended prophylaxis is not universally recommended.
In medical intensive care unit (ICU) patients, risk factors 
often overlap and VTE risk is high. Meta-analyses show 
that heparin-based prophylaxis (either LMWH or unfrac-
tionated heparin) effectively reduces symptomatic PE and 
DVT in this population.
692 Consequently, prophylactic 
anticoagulation is recommended in Western guidelines.692,693 
However, adding IPC to pharmacologic prophylaxis has 
not shown additional benefit.
694 Similarly, no studies have 
demonstrated the effectiveness of adding elastic stockings 
to anticoagulation, even in surgical patients.
695,696
Because medical ICU patients often have high bleeding 
risks, mechanical prophylaxis (e.g., IPC) is widely used in 
Japan in place of anticoagulation. Although RCTs have 
not definitively demonstrated the efficacy of IPC in ICU 
patients,
697 a network meta-analysis including 5 RCTs 
(one of which was negative) found that IPC significantly 
reduced the incidence of VTE compared with no prophylaxis 
or the use of elastic stockings alone.
578,698
XIII. COVID-19 and Venous Thromboembolism (Recommendation Table  37)
PM?s??u??
Thromboembolism in COVID-19
COVID-19 is an acute viral pulmonary disease caused by 
severe acute respiratory syndrome coronavirus 2 (SARS-
CoV-2). Although the primary clinical manifestation is 
respiratory infection, COVID-19 has been reported to 
have a high rate of thrombotic complications, especially an 
elevated risk of venous thromboembolism (VTE), including 
pulmonary embolism (PE).
699,700
Although the exact pathogenesis of VTE in COVID-19 
remains unclear, several mechanisms have been proposed, 
including: development of disseminated intravascular 
coagulation due to severe infection;
701 the frequent need 
for intensive care unit (ICU) management; 702 and the 
effects of various COVID-19 therapies on platelets and 
coagulation.
703,704
A Chinese observational study reported that 20 of 81 
ICU patients with severe COVID-19 developed VTE 
(25%).
705 In an observational study conducted in the 
Netherlands, VTE occurred in 31% of 184 patients with 
severe COVID-19.
706 Prophylactic anticoagulation therapy 
had not been administered in any of these patients. The 
wide variation in reported VTE incidence has been 
attributed to potential differences depending on whether 
the subjects were ICU or non-ICU patients, and whether 
Recommendation Table 37. Recommendations and Levels of Evidence Regarding the Association Between 
COVID-19, Thrombosis, and VTE
COR LOE
 Clinicians should be careful of thrombosis as a cardiovascular complication when treating patients
 
with COVID-19 I B
 Uniform anticoagulation therapy for patients with mild (no pneumonia) or moderate (no oxygen 
administration) COVID-19 is not recommended
III
No benefit B
 Anticoagulation therapy for patients with moderate (oxygen administration) or severe (ICU 
admission or mechanical ventilation) COVID-19 should be considered IIa B
COR, Class of Recommendation; ICU, intensive care unit; LOE, Level of Evidence; VTE, venous thromboembolism.
Advance Publication
[[p65]]
65
JCS/JPCPHS 2025 Guideline on PH and VTE
imaging screening was performed. Specifically, the incidence 
was 40.3% and 9.5% with and without screening ultraso-
nography, respectively, and 22.7% and 7.9% among ICU 
and non-ICU patients, respectively.
707
2. Epidemiology in Japan
According to a joint questionnaire sent out by the Japanese 
Society of Phlebology and the Pulmonary Embolism 
Research Group in August 2020, VTE was reported in 7 
(0.6%) COVID-19 patients.
708 Of these, 5 (0.4%) had PE. 
All 5 patients were critically ill, requiring mechanical 
ventilation during hospitalization.
709 A joint survey in 
December 2020 by the Japanese Society on Thrombosis 
and Hemostasis, the Japan Atherosclerosis Society, and a 
Ministry of Health research group reported that of 5,687 
hospitalized COVID-19 patients in 109 hospitals, 105 
(1.85%) developed thrombotic events: DVT in 41 (0.72%) 
and PE in 29 (0.51%).
710 In an analysis focusing only on 
those who underwent contrast-enhanced computed tomog-
raphy, VTE was found in 10 of 45 patients (22.2%). Notably, 
none of the patients with mild COVID-19 had VTE, 
whereas 40% of patients with severe COVID-19 did.
711
The CLOT-COVID registry, Japan’s largest registry on 
thrombosis and anticoagulation in COVID-19 patients, 
reported 55 (1.9%) thrombotic events among 2,894 patients 
hospitalized between April and September 2021.
712 The 
incidence of thrombotic events increased with disease 
severity: 0.2%, 1.4%, and 9.5% in mild, moderate, and 
severe disease, respectively. Overall, thrombotic events in 
Japan were much lower than in international reports, but 
were present in a considerable proportion of patients with 
severe disease.
3. Anticoagulation Therapy and Primary 
Prevention of Thrombosis
Given the high thrombotic tendency and risk of VTE in 
COVID-19 patients, several international societies have 
issued statements recommending universal anticoagulation 
therapy for all hospitalized patients to prevent thrombosis 
and improve prognosis.
713,714 Various studies have examined 
the optimal intensity of anticoagulation (prophylactic vs. 
therapeutic dose). Initial reports were skeptical about the 
benefits of full-dose therapy,
715–717 but more recent large-
scale studies have demonstrated its efficacy in moderately 
ill COVID-19 patients.
718
In Japan, given the low thrombotic risk in mild cases of 
COVID-19 and the potentially higher bleeding risk in Asian 
compared with Western populations, caution is required.
719 
Unlike other countries where low-molecular-weight heparin 
is commonly used, only unfractionated heparin has been 
approved for VTE prophylaxis in Japan. Therefore, based 
on domestic data, several Japanese societies jointly issued 
a guideline titled Clinical Practice Guidelines for Thrombosis 
Prevention and Anticoagulant Therapy in Novel Coronavirus 
Disease (COVID-19).
720
